[trialAccount t18470614-1487]
1487. WILLIAM GEEVES was indicted for stealing 1 half-crown, 2 shillings, and 6 sixpences; the monies of Charles Bunn, from the person of Elle Bunn; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ELLEN BUNN . I am the wife of Charles Bunn, and live at No. 70, Brunswick-street, Hackney-road. I have not been with my husband lately. On the 7th of June I went to the Gun public-house, in Shoreditch, at two o'clock in the morning—I went into the parlour, and remained there till near five—I went there to see for my husband—he drinks very much, and I expected he would be there—I came out of the parlour about five o'clock, and stood by the bar-door—the prisoner was sitting close against me, with a female and two or three men—he got up and called me by name, and asked how I was—I looked at him, and said. "Very well, thank you"—he came and took the glass that I had, and drank out of it—I felt him pull my dress, and I missed half-a-crown, two shillings, and six sixpences—they were in my pocket in a paper—I saw the paper in his hand—I pulled a piece of it off, and gave it to the landlord, and he threw it down in front of the bar—I am sure it was the paper the money was in—there was the mark of the money on the paper—I took it up—the prisoner and the others got it out of my hand and destroyed it—before they destroyed it I had ascertained that it was the paper that had had my money—I told the prisoner he had robbed me—he said, "Oh, nonsense."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What is your husband? A. An omnibus coachman—we do not live apart, but they do not allow him to come in at all hours of the night, which he is in the habit of doing—I get my living by work, and what I get from my friends—I went to the public-house about two o'clock, to see my husband, and I remained there till five—before two I had come from home—I was not out the night before—I was out the night after—I am not often out of a night—I am, when it suits me to go out—the prisoner was not so drunk as he appeared to be—he went out and crossed on the other side of the way, but I had accused him of robbing me before that—I said to the prisoner, "Don't rob me," and he said, "Oh, nonsense"—a man
said, "I robbed you," and I said, "Yes, you did"—that was before the prisoner robbed me—the money I lost was in my pocket, in front of my dress—my husband told me to prosecute the prisoner, and he said he would run me through with a knife if I did not prosecute—I do not believe that my husband went afterwards to the prisoner's brother, and tried to get money—my husband drinks a little, but he would not encourage robbing—I am a respectable hard-working woman—on the night after this I went to my sister's, in Hoxton, as I do when I have not what I want at home, but they will not keep my husband—I very seldom go to concerts—I have been two or three times to Grove's Concert-rooms—I never had my face blackened in my life—I have gone home shortly after the concerts have been over—I have gone home with my husband, most times—I never went with any person in place of my husband—I have never taken any one home with me—I do not help to get my living by going with other persons—I never go home with other men—my home is too respectable, through it is poor—I did not like the idea of prosecuting—I never was before a Magistrate in my life—I stopped in the public-house from two till five, by myself—I interfered with no one—I was anxious to see my husband—we have been in apartments lately, which is not like having our own house—the people in the house will not allow my husband, as he gets tipsy, to come home at all hours in the night—it is not at all agreeable, and I objected to it—I do not like any one to make a disturbance—I went to the public-house because my husband gets in there with bad company, treating them with drink; and I was anxious to see whether he was there or not—I was at home the night before, and the night after—I slept at home in my own bed—I had been out in the evening, but I came home about one or two o'clock—I did not go out at all the night after that—I do not often go out—my usual time of going to bed is eleven, if I have work to do I sit up till twelve.
Q. Did you not once say you had been robbed of some money in a public-house, and was not that money found when the house was swept? A. Not till a man came to me, and said, "What money have you in your pocket?"—I said, "I think I have two or three half-crowns"—he told me to feel, and I had not got the money—he went to two or three men, and they said, "You must hold the door, and say you will give him into custody, or you won't get it;" and then the man that I felt touch my dress, said he would go and look in the dust, and he found one half-crown, but I saw him put it back into the dust out of the sleeve of his coat—I saw it plainly—I did not see my husband on this morning when I was robbed—I saw him in the middle of the day—the prisoner was standing close by me, and I felt his hand in my pocket—I went to him, and said, "You have robbed me," and I saw the paper in his hand—I pulled a piece of it out of his hand—I gave it to the landlord, and he threw it down—I went to pick it up again, and some of the prisoner's connexion tore it out of my hand, and passed another piece of paper to the prisoner, and he said, "This is what I had," but I knew it was nothing of the kind—I was not tipsy nor stupid—I had a little gin-and-water at the Gun—I did not leave my house till twelve at night—I went to my husband—he was going home with the last omnibus—he wanted me to ride, but I said no I would walk—I went to the public-house, but people do not go to a public-house to be murdered—I was knocked down there—I pulled part of the paper out of the prisoner's hand, and I saw the other piece remaining in his hand, but whether the money remained in that, I cannot tell—it was a piece of whiteybrown paper, a piece of a flour-bag, and on that portion I got I
saw the print of the money—I had brought the money from home—I had received a parcel from home, which I do every week; and I received a half-sovereign in the parcel, from my mother—she sends me half-a-sovereign a week, which is very little—if I want more I can have it—I will swear my mother sent me the half-sovereign—I had changed it in the day—I do not know where I changed it—my mother lives at St. Alban's—the money came by the coach—there was some pork, and some bread, and a great many other things in the parcel.
FREDERICK SCOTT . I live in Winkworth-buildings, Hoxton, and am a gasman. I went into the Gun public-house, about five o'clock in the morning. on the 7th of June—the prisoner and other persons were there—I saw the prosecutrix standing at the bar—the prisoner was close to her—I saw some fellows chuck her under the chin, and the prisoner immediately left her side and walked close to me—I noticed a piece of brown paper in his hand—the prosecutrix went to him, accused him of robbing her, and snatched the piece of paper out of his hand—she said that was the very piece of paper her money was wrapped up in—she gave the piece of paper to the landlord, and he threw it down—I saw somebody give the prisoner another piece of paper, and he said, "This is the very piece I had in my hand"—the prisoner went out, and Mr. Bunn followed him, and gave him in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. How long did the prisoner remain in the public-house after the piece of paper was put into his hand? A. Twenty minutes, or half-an-hour—I was going by there on my way home—I work for the City Gas Company—I went there to give a fellow-servant of mine something to drink, to help me to do my work that morning—I did not see any money, I only saw Mr. Bun snatch a bit of paper from the prisoner when he was close to me.
CHARLES RAYMAN ( police-constable H 95.) At a little before six o'clock that morning I was on duty in Shoreditch—I met the prosecutrix outside the public-house—she charged the prisoner with having robbed her of 7 s. 6 d.—I took him into custody—I told him what I wanted him for.
Cross-examined. Q. He had got across the road? A. Yes—I saw him on the other side of the way—I went and took him by the arm and said, "I want you for picking this person's pocket of 7 s. 6 d."—he said, "Very well"—I found on him 4 1/2 d. in copper, and a sixpence in silver.
MR. PAYNE to ELLEN BUNN. Q. Did you leave your parasol at the Gun for a quartern of gin? A. I wanted to leave it, because I did not want to show my money, because I had been robbed there before—I just put the parasol over the bar—I told the landlord I had money, but I did not want to show it.
HENRY BEAN . I was a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted, on the 23 rd of Aug., 1841, and confined ten days)—the prisoner is the person—I have known him ever since—he has been in custody a great many times.
GUILTY .— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18470614-1488]
1488. HENRY BRADY was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 1 l.; 1 pair of trowsers, 16 s.; 1 waistcoat, 6 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, 3 s.; 1 brooch, 1 s.; sovereigns and 1 half-sovereign; the property of George Hines; in the dwelling-house of Henry Huline.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE HINES . I am potman at the Clay Hall public-house at Old Ford. On the 3rd of May, about seven o'clock in the evening I went to my bed-room, which is over the bar—I saw my box was safe and locked up—it contained 7 l. 10 s. in gold and silver, a coat, a pair of trowsers, and a waistcoat—I went out, and returned about twenty-five minutes past eight o'clock—I then saw my bedroom door was open—I went up and saw my box was open and the money and clothes were gone—I know the prisoner—he formerly lived fellow-servant in the house with me—he was in the habit of coming to the house afterwards—he knew where I kept my money as well as I did—I suspected him, and went in search of him—I did not find him, but he came to me on the Wednesday following, between nine and ten o'clock, and asked if I wanted him—I said, "Yes," and he walked up to the station-house with me—I had been making such a search for him on Monday and Tuesday that he would be likely to hear of it—he was discharged on the next day.
SARAH HUNT was called on her recognizance, and did not appear.
ANN REEVES . I am the wife of Joseph Reeves. On the night of the 3rd of May, about a quarter past eight o'clock, I was at the bar of the Clay Hall public-house—I saw the prisoner come from the back part of the premises—he went to the tap-room—I did not see him leave.
THOMAS ROGERS . I am a dyer. On Monday night, the 3rd of May, about twenty minutes past eight o'clock, I was coming home from my work at Mr. Parnell's, the dyer—I saw the prisoner about 100 yards from the Clay Hall public-house, with two young men—I said to him, "There you are, Harry"—he did not make me any answer—I spoke loud enough for him to hear me—I was close to him—he had a bundle under his coat—it did not look very large, but it would, I dare say, contain a coat, waistcoat, and pair of trowsers—he was stooping down under the fence, and they went through a gate into a field where there is no thoroughfare.
Prisoner. Q. When I saw you, did I not have a jacket and cap on? A. No, you had a blue coat and a bundle—you were coming from towards the house—you saw me, and then you turned towards the house.
ROBERT EDWARDS . I am brother to the landlord of the Waterman's Arms public-house, in Fore-street, Limehouse. On the morning of the 4th of May, about half-past one or two o'clock, I saw Sarah Hunt and the prisoner at our house, which is about two miles from the Clay Hall—they staid till five o'clock in the morning—the prisoner paid for what they had with a half-sovereign.
WILLIAM KING . I am assistant to Mr. Williams', a pawnbroker, in High-street, Stepney—I have a coat, pawned about twelve o'clock on the 4th of May for 8 s., by Sarah Hunt—she went out to speak to a person outside, who she said was her brother, that the coat belonged to—I did not see the person.
ZEPHANIAH TAYLOR , ( police-constable K 230.) I am stationed at Bow. On Friday, the 7th of May, I saw the prisoner in Mr. Bird's brick-field at Old Ford—I went to the edge of the pit where he was at work—he saw me, and asked if I wanted him—I said, "Yes"—he put on his clothes and came away—he asked if there was any further evidence against him—I told him I did not know—when we got to the station, I found the female Hunt had been apprehended—she was before the Magistrate—I have since tried in every place to find her, but I cannot—when I told the prisoner at the station that there was a woman apprehended, he said, "If there is a woman come to swear against me I shall get seven years, and if I get that, I will ask for life"—Hunt was afterwards discharged, and gave her evidence.
HENRY HAWKINS . I am a pawnbroker. I produce a waistcoat and a pair of trowsers, pawned by Sarah Hunt for 10 s.
GEORGE HINES re-examined. These clothes are mine.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1489]
1489. ISAAC ELKIN was indicted for stealing 60 pieces of silk handkerchiefs, value 62 l.; the goods of Henry Tucker and others:—2nd Count, calling them 420 silk handkerchiefs.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM KAYESS . I am in the service of Henry Tucker and others, silk mercers, in Trump-street. On the 26th of April the prisoner came to the warehouse—he wanted sixty pieces of Korahs (that is East India silk handkerchiefs) for a shipping order to Hamburgh; and he wished to have them on approbation for the approval of a third party, if he chose forty or fifty pieces out of them he would return them to us to ship, and the others were to be returned to us—this is the invoice that was sent with the goods.
COURT. Q. This invoice says, "Bought on approbation;" that is, that the party buying had a right to reject? A. Yes.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was it quite understood that these were to be returned for shipment, supposing they were bought? A. Yes; I did not sell them outright to the prisoner, but we keep no memorandum-book in our establishment—we keep no waste-book, therefore we send out an invoice of this sort, and put on it "On approbation"—if the prisoner had wanted to purchase for himself I should not have let him had them—I had no authority to let him have them—I was not to be brought in communication with the third party; but some of these were to be selected, then returned to me, and I was to ship them—our terms for these goods were nett cash—it was understood by me that the prisoner wished to have them for about a couple of days—part of them would have been rejected, or the whole might have been kept—in either case we should have shipped them—I should not have parted with them except on those terms—I directed them to be sent to Bucklers-bury with the invoice—I went there about four days afterwards, and found the prisoner's premises were closed—I was not able to find the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. How long have you been in your employer's service? A. Fifteen years—I have known the prisoner two or three years, from having had a few dealings with him at our premises—the first time was in 1844, to the amount of 160 l.—we had dealing with him up to the time of this transaction—this is the first transaction that has not been paid, to my knowledge—we never applied to him for custom to my knowledge—I cannot swear we have not—nett cash is cash from seven to ten days—the bill for these goods ( looking at one) is dated 13th October; it was paid by a check, dated 11th November—I should not call that cash—our clerk who made the entry in the book is not here—this might be a matter of accommodation—I cannot tell what our clerk would do—goods very rarely come back to us to be shipped—I never had such a transaction myself before—there is no drawback allowed on these goods—when the prisoner came to me, I demurred to let him have the goods on approbation—I wished him to have them in the usual way—it is not the custom of our house to send goods out on approbation—if he had bought them in the usual way I would have let him had them—he might have had dealings with us ten days before this—these are our invoices—I have all the books here—they are in our clerk's writing—we have nothing to do with the book or money transactions—we
let persons have goods and call for payment on the second Saturday afterwards—the prisoner said he wanted these goods for two or three days, and then they were to come back to us to ship—they were 21 s. 6 d. a-piece—they would be perhaps 1 s. a-piece dearer if wanted for home consumption——the goods are here in custody of the Sheriff of London—Mr. Pattison produced ten pieces of them at the Mansion-house—I did not find the prisoner in Bucklersbury—I found a reference there to his solicitor, Mr. Parry—I went there—Mr. Parry opened his book and read a memorandum, and I went to Mr. Pattison but did not see him.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Supposing goods were bought for shipment, would they remain on your premises? A. They would go to the East India ware-house, and remain some days before they went—we should take care of their going abroad—by the prisoner representing he wanted these on approbation, he got them off the premises at once—I should not have suffered them to leave the warehouse unless he had represented they were for approbation.
MICHAEL WILLS . I am warehouseman to Messrs. Tucker and Co. I packed up these goods and delivered them to Cliff.
HENRY CLIFF . I took these goods to Mr. Elkin's, No. 38, Bucklersbury—there was no appearance of shutting up the shop then.
JAMES PATTISON . I am an agent for Irish linens—I have an office at No. 2, Church-passage, Guildhall. On the 27th of April I received seventy pieces of Korah silk handkerchiefs from the prisoner—I gave him 21 s. 6 d. a-piece for them—I had dealings with him before—he owed me better than 100 l.—these handkerchiefs went in discharge of the debt—he gave me them, and a check for 22 l.—these goods have been since seized by the Sheriff—I had made a contract with the prisoner about my receiving these goods—about the beginning of April he delivered five pieces as a sample—I expected three on the 27th of April—I meant to buy them of him—I gave him an order for 100 pieces.
COURT. Q. Did you mean to export these? A. yes: I got them for an export house.
MR. BALLANTINE here withdrew from the prosecution.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1490]
1490. WILLIAM KYNASTON and FREDERICK MILLER were indicted for stealing 8 lbs. weight of flour, value 4 s.; the goods of the Eastern Countries Railway Company, their masters.
MR. BALLABTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN FINLAYSON . I am out-of-door superintendent of the goods department of the Eastern Countries Railway, in Brick-lane. The prisoners were coal-porters there—they were ordered into the granary to sweep it up—on the 21st and 22nd of May there was a great quantity of flour there belonging to different consignees—I ordered Dyson t watch the prisoner.
WILLIAM DYSON . I am a porter in the employ of the Eastern Counties Railway Company. On the morning of the 22nd of May I concealed myself behind a sack of flour in the flour-warehouse—after remaining there about two hours the prisoners came—they walked across the floor of the ware-house to find a broom—they could not find any—they went to the upper-floor and came down with brooms in their hands—they went and opened the inner door of the granary, and walked from thence to where this flour was—Kynaston walked by the sacks as they stood—Miller called him back, and
said, "Stop; here is a sack"—Kynaston went back to him—they undid the sack—it was loose but not opened—they opened it, and each of them took flour out, and put it into a bag or cloth—each of them had some—they put it under their clothes and went away—I went to the sack and found it was pulled together, but not tied—it had been tied before—I missed from 8lbs. to 9lbs. of flour from it.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. What size is this granary? A. A very large place—the prisoners came for a morning or two to sweep it—I do not know how long they had been in the employ of the Company—I was on the top of one sack and behind another—I was higher than the Prisoners—I could have reached them with my hand—I followed them till I met the foreman—they went down stairs, and I lost sight of them for three or four minutes—they were not taken into custody in my presence—about two hours elapsed before they were taken—this sack was loose, but it was not open—I had not measured or weighed the flour—I judged of what had been taken, by the hole that was made—there were hundreds of sacks there—I had examined this sack that morning—we set it there on purpose, and the string was cut loose on purpose—I believe it was laid like a trap.
SAMUEL GREEN ( police-constable H 61.) On the 22nd of May the prisoners were brought to the station—there was nothing found on them; but Kynaston's waistcoat was stained white with flour—this is the waistcoat—I went to the prisoners' houses—I found at Miller's two dishes of flour, which weighed 8lbs. 6 ounces.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did you not find some flour bags also? A. No.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. You found no marks on Miller? A. No; only on Kynaston—this waistcoat was under his smock-frock—he stated the way it got stained, was by sweeping the floor—I have had the waistcoat ever since—it is exactly in the state it was—I found no flour at Kynaston's.
JAMES HUDSON SPARKS . I am a foreman at the Railway. I gave the prisoners into custody—I observed that Kynaston's waistcoat was stained with flour—I asked the prisoners where they lived—I was present when the flour was found at Miller's—I have not compared it with the flour in the sack it was taken from.
RANSOM READ . I am delivery foreman at the Eastern Railway Company's warehouse, in Brick-lane. Dyson pointed out a sack to me—I weighed it—it was 2cwt. 1 qr. 17lbs.; the proper weight was 2 cwt. 1qr. 25 lbs.—I took a sample of it to Worship-street, and compared it with the flour in the dishes—I cannot say that it was the same; it was a similar sample.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. What are you? A. I was bred and born a miler—I know this granary—I am there seven times a-week—there are about 1,500 sacks on the floor—I took three samples out of three sacks that had been robbed by somebody—the flour found in the dishes were like two of the samples—it was not like the third, that was another man's flour.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1491]
1491. WILLIAM COOK was indicted for feloniously assaulting Elizabeth Cook, and cutting and wounding her in and upon head, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
MESSRS. BALLANTINE and THOMSON conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH COOK . I live at No. 90, Drury-lane, the prisoner is my husband. On the 20th of May he came there with a stick in his hand—he held the stick over his head to strike me—I rose from the bed I was on, I laid hold of the stick, and Phœbe Morgan and I got the stick from him—he then took a knife off the table—I went into the bed-room and shut the door—he forced the door open, and rushed into the room; he threw me down first, and then stabbed me in the back of my head with the knife—I held his hand with both my hands, and he could not get his hand away, and he screwed my face round with the nails of his other hand—he scratched me and hurt me terribly—he heard some footsteps coming, dropped the knife by the side of the bed and left me—Phœbe Morgan stopped him from going down stairs—he went up stairs, and was taken on the top stairs of the house—he was quite sober—we had been parted twelve months—he had threatened the day before that he would come with a knife.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. He had seen you before this? A. Yes; he said, "You have had your frolic, but you don't see Whit-Sunday thought it is very close to it"—we had been married thirteen years—he had only come out of the workhouse the day before—I had a daughter by the prisoner, she is eleven years old, she lives with me—I carry on business in the name of Cogan—I write up "Cogan late Cook"—I am not living with any man—Mr. Cogan used to come backward and forward, the same as any of the work people—my husband in general has a stick—the knife was in the room—it was about ten minutes before twelve o'clock in the day when he came—I was in bed, because he had threatened my life the day before, and I was up the greater part of the night—I had seen Mr. Cogan on the Monday before—he came to tell me my husband had come out—my husband raised the stick—I got it from him before any blow was struck—I have been carrying on business with "Cogan late Cook" written up, for twelve months—my husband left me in the street with a child—I did not go to the place, take the rent-money out of the drawer, and then send in a broker—the prisoner did not say he did not come to Kick up a row, but to see after his daughter—he did not say anything of the kind there; he did at Bow-street—I did not strive him first—Morgan and I got the stick from him—Morgan had brought the Knife into the room and laid it on the table.
Q. Will you swear she had not the Knife in her hand, and you were all struggling, and so you got the cut? A. I can swear she had not the Knife in hand; I saw him take it up.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you been cohabiting with Cogan? A. No, no more than the protection of the name—I have been obliged to work for myself, getting no assistance from my husband for twelve months—I lost a great deal of blood, but the wound was not a large one.
PHŒBE MORGAN . I was at work at Mr. Cook's when her husband came—she was in bed—he had a stick in his hand—he went through the shop—I told him there was no one at home—he went to the bed-room—he held up the stick to hit my mistress—I took the stick from him and broke it—he ran to the table, took up the Knife, and stabbed my mistress on the back of her head, and scratched her face—my mistress is a glass-grinder—Mr. Cogan is my master—he brings work there—he does not live in the house—there was no man in bed with Mr. Cook.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you break the stick over the prisoner's back? A. No; he clung to the stick, and broke it into three or four pieces—he had it over his head to strike.
LOUISA MOORE . I saw the prisoner come in with a Knife, the day before he said he would take away Mr. Cook's life—he said none of us should see Whitsuntide—I saw him come up stairs on the day this occurred—I saw Mr. Cook bleeding from the back part of her head—I went to the doctor's.
ARTHUR KIRKMAN ( police-constable F 20.) I took the prisoner—I asked him how he came to stab his wife in the back of her head—he told me he did not do it—on the way to the station he told me he did do it on account of going home and finding another man in bed with Mr. Cook—I found this Knife on the ground and this stick on the table.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say that Mr. Morgan did it A. Yes; he did afterwards—I found him sitting on the top step of the stairs.
JOHN PHILLIPS . I am assistant to Mr. Walker, a surgeon in Drury-lane, On the 20th of May Mr. Cook came to me; her face was smeared with blood, and there was a small scalp wound at the back of her head—it was such a wound as this Knife would have made—it had left off bleeding when I saw her.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 45.— confined One Year, and to enter into recognizances for his good behaviour for five years.
THIRD COURT, Monday, June 21 st, 1847.
PRESENT —Mr. Alderman HOOPER; Mr. Alderman WILLIAM HUNTER; Mr. Alderman SIDNEY; Mr. Alderman MOON; and EDWARD BULLOCK, Esq.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

1493. MARY GORDON was indicted for stealing 1 sovereign, 3 half-crowns, 1 shilling, 1 sixpence, and 1 piece of foreign copper coin, value 1/4 d.; the monies of John Harber Barber, from his person; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN HARBER BARBER . I live in Brewer-street, Pimlico. On the 12th of June, about twenty minutes past ten at night, I met the prisoner at Buckingham-gate—she asked me if I would give her a drop of gin—I took her into the White Horse—we had a quartern of gin, came out together, and came down St. James'-street together, walking side by side till we came to William-street, when she turned up a dark passage—I put my hand in my pocket, missed my money, and followed her—she met me, and said there was no thoroughfare, I could not go through—I accused her of robbing me—she denied it—a policeman came, and I gave her in charge—I had had one sovereign, three half-crowns, and one shilling and sixpence loose in my pocket—it was my wages which I had received, and an old coin which I had found three weeks ago—this now produced is it—I swear to it by this figure "7" on it.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you want to take improper liberties with me? A. No—I did not offer you sixpence, or pull up your clothes.
THOMAS BROWNE ( police-constable.) I took the prisoner in charge from the prosecutor—he had been drinking, but knew perfectly well what he was about—the place is no thoroughfare—I got a lamp, made a search, but found nothing—next morning, between three and four o'clock, I searched again—I saw two half-crowns in a piece of rag, pulled out from behind an old board, ten or twelve yards from the end of the street.
MARY ANN BARBER . I am the prosecutor's wife—as soon as it was light in the morning I went with the constable, made a search, put my hand in between the gutter and boards, and pulled out the rag—it is an old white pocket-handkerchief.
ELIZA JOHNSON . I search females at the Westminster Police-station. On Saturday night, about eleven o'clock, I searched the prisoner, and found this coin in a pair of mittens which she had on, and a shilling, a sixpence, and a halfpenny in her pocket—after I undressed her completely, I said, "What have you got in your hand"—she said "Only an old dirty glove"—I do not know where she took it from, but I think it was from her bosom—I found this half-crown in it.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I put it on the table? A. No. Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor had a hole in his waistcoat pocket; at the public-house there was a large nail hanging out; a farthing dropped out; he said I might keep it for a keepsake.
THOMAS PRONGER ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction from Westminster—( read—convicted August 1842, and confined three months)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY .** Aged 39.— Transported for Seven Years.

1494. SUTHERLAND MARSHALL was indicted for stealing 14lbs. weight of copper, value 8 s.; the goods of Edward French, in a vessel in a port of entry and discharge.
JAMES COLLINS . I am a labourer in the London Dock. On the morning of the 16th of June, I was at work on board the ship Symmetry —I saw the prisoner on board—I saw him lift the lid of a chest up, take out some copper, put it into a piece of gunnebag, and hand it over the ship's side to another man, who took it from him—I called to the mate, who went after him.
Prisoner Q. Did you see me take the copper from the man again? A. No.
WILLIAM JAMES . I was second mate of the Symmetry, and have since been discharged. In consequence of what Collins told me I went after the prisoner—the policeman brought him back to me—he had the copper under his arm—he said it was given to him—I knew it directly—it belonged to the ship—the master is Edward French.
ROBERT MANNING ( police-constable.) I took the prisoner in charge—I found this copper under his arm, wrapped up in a piece of gunnebag —I asked what he had got, he said he did not know—I asked how he came by it, he said a person gave it him to carry.
Prisoner's Defence. A man asked me to carry a parcel into the East India Docks, and wait till he came; I did not know what I had.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 30. Recommended to mercy.— Confined Four Months.

1495. JOHN JONES was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 2 l. 6 s.; the goods of James William Benson.
WILLIAM BENSON . I am an agent, and live in south-street, Southwark-square.
On the 16th of June, about ten minutes past nine o'clock in the evening, I went into the shop of my son James William Benson, a silversmith and jeweler, on Cornhill—I saw the prisoner sitting in a chair at the counter—my son was behind the counter, showing him some watches—the prisoner said he should not have the chain that night, but would come back to-morrow evening—I sat in the chair next him—he had watches in his hand several times—he rose, said, "I shall take this," took a watch in his left hand, and ran out of the shop on to the pavement of the Royal Exchange—I called, "Stop thief!"—my son caught him—I called the young man out of the shop to assist his master, as I was afraid to leave—he went out—as we were bringing the prisoner across the road the watch fell, or was thrown from him—my son picked it up, gave it to me, and I gave it to the policeman—this produced is it—the prisoner was given in charge—he said, "You Have found no watch on me"—it had been dropped then.
JOSEPH DAVIES . I am shop man to Mr. Benson—I heard the call of "Stop thief," went after the prisoner, and helped to secure him—I saw the watch picked up—this produced is it.
EDWARD HOPDUP ( City policeman.) I took the prisoner in custody—the watch was given me by Mr. Benson.
Prisoner. I plead guilty.
GUILTY. Aged 21. Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1496]
1496. WILLIAM HUNT was indicted for stealing 1 pint of linseed oil, value 3 d.; the goods of Joseph Gordon Davis, his master.
JOSEPH GORDONO DAVIS . I am a builder, and live at Pimlico. On the 14th of June, in the morning, I found this bottle of oil secreted in an iron cistern on my premises—I suspected a man named Cotterell, but about twelve o'clock I saw the prisoner coming out of the front door of my house with this oil—there is but one door—I had seen him three or four minutes previously at the part of the house where the oil was—I taxed him with it—he pulled it out of his pocket, and said he brought it from home—I said, "It is false, it is my oil"—he said his wife gave it him, that she bought it at Roberts's in Store-street—I went with him to Roberts's, and sent for his wife—both his wife and the shopman in his presence denied all knowledge of it—I sent for a policeman, he had not told a lie, he said "Yes"—the bottle is his own.
Prisoner. Q. How can you prove I saw you there, and at No. 14, in the next street, there was a trace where somebody had emptied oil—I did not see you take it.
ANDREW SWANTON CRIPPS ( policeman.) The prisoner was given in my charge—the oil was given me by Mr. Davis—as I went to the station with the prisoner, he told me he had to oil some paintings for his mother, and that it was his own—he did not say how he came by it—at the station he said he had bought it of Mr. Scott, at Walliam-green, on Friday evening last.
CAROLINE SCOTT . I live at Walliam-green, Fulham—I saw the prisoner on Friday—he had some oil and turpentine of me mixed together, but I did not sell him any plain oil.
Prisoner's Defence. The bottle of oil belongs to me; I bought this oil on Thursday, and emptied it into a jar; it was on Friday night that I bought some oil and turps.
JOSEPH GORDON DAVIS re-examined. I can swear this is pure oil.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined One Month.

1497. MARY ANN HOY was jndicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 30 s.; 1 bonnet, 10 s.; 2 petticoats, 2 s.; 2 pairs of drawers, 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 pair of boots, 2 s.; the goods of Esther Cohen: 1 shawl, 30 s.; 1 gown, 4 s.; and 1 handkerchief, 6 d.; the goods of Charlotte Cohen, her mistress.
CHARLOTTE CHHEN . I am single, and live in New Gravel-lane. The prisoner was my servant—on the 28th of May I told her to get up and open the shop—in about half an hour my sister came down, and told me something—I found that the prisoner was gone—I had not told her to go—my boxes were broken open—they had been shut—one was locked the night before—all the things were lying about the floor—I missed two shawls, a dress, two petticoats, and a silk handkerchief—one shawl belonged to Esther Cohen, my sister—she lived with me—this shawl produced is hers—the other things are mine—I have had this dress three years—I had only bought the shawls on Monday—I made these two petticoats—I had not had them a week in the house—they had been safe in the boxes—we went after the prisoner about eight o'clock in the morning, and caught her at Barking about five o'clock in the evening.
THOMAS JOHN BLAKE ( police-constable K 137.) I went with the prosecutrix to Barking, took the prisoner, and told her I wanted her, for stealing things at Cohen's, in New Gravel-lane—she said, "Yes, I stole the things; I will take you where they are"—she took me to her house, in Barking—I found some there—she put her hand into her pocket, and gave this duplicate for some of the things.
Prisoner. I have lived with those girls seven months, and kept them by my prostitution.
CHARLOTTE COHAN re-examined. It is not so, on my oath.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1498]
1498. SELINA ALLEN was indicted for stealing 2 coats, value 1 l. 5 s.; 1 set of curtains, 8 s.; 3 yards of silk, 7 s.; seven Knives, 18 d.; 7 forks, 1 s.; 2 pairs of shoes, 1 l. 8 s.; 1 waistcoat, 2 s.; 4 blinds, 16 s.; and 1 brush; 1 s.; the goods of George Macnalley, her master.
GEORGE MACNALLEY . I live in Park-street, Camden-town. The prisoner was in my employ six years—about a month ago I missed a pair of boots—they were my son's, George Robert Macnalley's—I asked the prisoner where my son's boots were—she said they were sent over to the shoemaker's to be repaired—I also missed my own boots—she told me she knew nothing of them—I told her if she did not tell me where they were I would call a policeman and take her up.
BENJAMIN BADDELEY ( police-constable S 248.). On the 4th of June I took the prisoner in charge at Mr. Macnalley's—I told her what it was for—she wished to go to the water-closet—I would not allow her—I followed her into a back room—the prosecutor said he should press the charge—I took the prisoner to the station—she was there searched by Mr. Doss, who gave me these duplicates.
ANN AMELIA DOSS . I live at the Police-station, in Albany-street—I search female prisoners. I searched the prisoner, and found forty-five duplicates in a bag, between her petticoat and her dress—she said she had pledged some, and another woman some—I gave them to Baddeley.
JAMES MARLOW . I live with Mr. Castle, a pawnbroker, in High-street, Camden-town. I produce a brush, pawned in Dec. last—this is the ticket of it—I produce a coat and some white window-curtains—I find duplicates of them.
JAMES JONES . I am in the service of Mr. James Hoby, of Camden-town, I produce a coat—I do not know who pledged it—this is the ticket I gave to the person—I produce a remnant of silk, some knives and forks, two pairs of shoes, a waistcoat, and some blinds, the tickets for which are here.
GEORGE MACNALLEY re-examined. The coat, curtains, silk, shoes, and waistcoat, are mine—these shoes are my son's.
Prisoner's Defence. I have lived with the prosecutor as a wife for nearly eight years; he has been aware of the things being in pledge; they were not pledged with the intention to rob him; my brother would have redeemed the things for me—part of them are my own.
GEORGE MACNALLEY re-examined. The prisoner was not living with me as my wife at this time, but she had been for about six years—she never had authority to pledge articles—she never told me she had pledged anything till after I found something out about my son's watch.
JURY. Q. What was the earliest date on which anything was found? A. The 2nd of March, 1846.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1499]
1499. FREDERICK JOHNSON was indicted for stealing 17lbs. weight of sugar, value 4 s.; the goods of James Huxtable, his master, in a vessel in a port of entry and discharge.
JOHN SUTTON . I am in the service of the East and West India Dock Company. On the 14th of June I stopped the prisoner going out of the Docks—a person with him was carrying his hammock, which was very heavy—I asked what was in it—the prisoner said his bed and his wet clothes—I put my hand in, and found they were dry—I slacked the lashing, pulled up the bed, and found this bag, with 17lbs. of raw sugar in it—he had got a pass for his things, from which I found he came from the ship Codrington —I went to the Codrington, and took the prisoner with me to Captain Huxtable, and in his presence showed the sample of sugar, and asked how he could think of taking that from the ship—it was part of the cargo—he said he did not know anything about it—the captain said he had nothing to do with it, and told me to do as I pleased—I took the prisoner to my superior, and then to the Thames Police-office—he was remanded—next day I examined some casks—there was one on the dock which had similar sugar in it—it had a great hole chopped in it, and a quantity of sugar taken—I examined it with what I found in the hammock, and found they did not agree—I then examined some casks on the quay, which I had just seen come ashore from the ship—I had seen them come over the ship's side, and know no others were there—I did not see them all landed—I examined the sugar in them, and it corresponded.
Q. What is the captain's name? A. James Huxtable, I believe—I said it was William at the Police-office—I was told so by one of the seamen, but afterwards ascertained that it was James.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not know it was in my hammock, I had just come home, and cut it down.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1500]
1500. JOHN PAYNE was indicted for stealing 4lbs. weight of flour, value 10 d.; the goods of Elijah Croucher, his master.
ELIJAH CROUCHER . I am a baker, and live in High-street, St. Giles's.—the prisoner has been in my employ. On the 14th of June, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, he went out—I followed, overtook him, and
charged him with having some flour—he admitted that he had it—he did not say how he came by it—I gave him charge—it is my property, and was in my bag, with my name on it—it is the sort of flour I had on my premises—he said if I would forgive him, he would not do it again.
Prisoner. You never followed me, you met me. Witness. I followed you—I did not go round Craven-street at all.
JOSEPH BASK ( Police-constable F. 124.) On the night of the 14th of June. the prisoner was given into my charge, by Croucher—I searched him, and found in each pocket a paper bag of flour, and a night-cap half full of flour, between his waistcoat and coat, which was buttoned up—I took him to the station—he said it was the first time, and he hoped his master would forgive him.
GUILTY. Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1501]
1501. HENRY STEVENS was indicted for unlawfully obtaining goods by false pretences.
WILLIAM BROCKING . I am in the service of William Watt, a grocer, of Park-terrace, Liverpool-road. On the 10th of May the prisoner came to the shop, he said he was in the service of William Hunt, and asked me to show him some shoe-brushes—I showed him a box containing several—he took three—he asked me the price—I said, "1 s. 4 d. each"—he said, "Put them down to my governor, you know who I mean"—I said, "You mean Hunt, across the way?"—he said. "Yes"—that is Mr. Hunt, a stable-keeper—he took the brushes away—on the afternoon of the same day he came again, and brought the brushes back, saying he would exchange them for three others, as Mr. Hunt supposed the first expense would be the least, and he would have a better quality—he then asked for a body-brush for the bodies and insides of carriages—he asked me the price—I told him they were marked outside—he had four brushes, three in exchange, and one extra—he said he would exchange them for Hunt, and that his master would come in the course of the week and settle for them—he took them away with him—he gave me his name, as Harry Smith—when he came a second time, he said, "It is customary for us men to have an invoice, merely to show our employer—I gave it him—on Friday, the 14th, he came again for a bottle of blacking—he took up a bottle of blacking, saying, "Add this to the brushes," and took it away.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are Messrs. Wood and Hunt in partnership? A. Yes, and were at this time—he said, "My governor," not "governors"—I am sure of that, because I said, "Hunt," and he said, "Yes"—I had seen him so often at Mr. Hunt's house, that I thought him the servant—I might have thought it suspicious if he had come the second time without returning the first set—it is very probable I might have asked him some question, or gone and made inquiry—he said, "No doubt Mr. Hunt will come on Saturday night, or to-morrow, or between this and Saturday, and pay for them"—Mr. Watt is in business alone—his Christian name is William.
JOHN COLLINS . I am a sergeant of police at Enfield. On Sunday, the 16th of May, between twelve and one o'clock at night, I saw the prisoner at a public-house at Enfield, quarrelling about a dog—I observed his person, and asked him what he had got in his pocket—he refused to tell me—he afterwards said they were brushes—I asked where he got them—he refused to ay—I took him to the station, and found four brushes and a bottle of
blacking in his pocket—he said he had purchased them to bring to Enfield to make them a present to his father; that he had been in the employ of a livery. stable keeper in the Liverpool-road, and had bought them at Mr. Watts' shop, he took the bill out of a purse in his pocket—I looked at it and said, "This is not a bill of these brushes, but a bill of Mr. Smith to Mr. Watts, and I know your name to be Stevens"—he said the master's name was Watts, and the man's name was Smith.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it true that he refused to tell you where he got them? A. Yes—he refused to give me a satisfactory account—I said so before the Magistrate, but do not see it in my depositions—it is not there.
GEORGE HUNT . I am a livery-stable keeper, and live at Islington. I also have two places, one in John-street, and the other in Barnsbury-street, Islington—the prisoner had been in my service a fortnight when he left me—I never sent him to Mr. Watts for blacking or brushes, or authorized him to get any.
Cross-examined. Q. If you wanted brushes would there be a great crime in a man going and getting them? A. Yes, without my orders—my men have not done so during the twelve years I have been in business—I never allow any one to get anything on credit, or in my name—Mr. Wood is in partnership with me—I call it my place, because I live there—my name is George.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Four Months.

[trialAccount t18470614-1504]
1504. HARRIET GRIFFIN and THOMAS COLE were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Elizabeth Mills, and stealing therein 2 sheets, value 3 s.; 1 blanket, 2 s.; 1 counterpane, 2 s.; 1 bolster, 1 s. 6 d.; 2 pillows, 2 s.; and 2 pillow-cases, 2 s., her goods; to which
GRIFFIN pleaded— GUILTY . Aged 33.— Confined Twelve Months.
LOUISA SARAH COWLES . I am single, and live at Old Nicholl-street, Bethnal-green—the house, No. 1, Maidstone-place, belongs to my mother, Maria Louisa Cowles—she is a widow—she does not live in the house—there are four rooms in it, which are let out in separate tenements—there is one passage to them—a person named Mills occupies the front parlour—the prisoner lived with us for six years, in a house in Shepherd's-court, opposite, which belongs to my mother also. On Wednesday, the 2nd of June, between one and two o'clock in the day, I went into Mr. Mills' room and missed a pair of sheets, which were my mother's property, from the bed—Mr. Mills had the use and care of them, they are not here—between five and six o'clock in the afternoon I supplied the bed with other sheets, I saw them safe between eight and nine o'clock, and fastened the room with a padlock—the prisoner Griffin held the light for me—I came away between eleven and
twelve o'clock the same night—in consequence of something I heard, I went into the room again, found the door open, the padlock on the floor, with the hasp of the lock on it, and the bed stripped—my mother sent for a policeman—I went to Shepherd's-court, where the prisoners lived, and in a water-closet there found a sheet, pillow-case, bolster, and apron—I did not see the apron when I was in the room, the pillow-case and everything else was there—these are the things which were found in the water-closet—they are my mother's property, and were let to Mr. Mills with the house—I found Griffin behind the door of another house, in the court, and Cole in a nook a little farther on—I and the constable spoke to them, then went to the station, gave information, and afterwards heard that Griffin was taken—I saw Cole taken, and went with him to the station.
ELIZA CLARK . I am the wife of George Clark, and have lived in Old Nicholl-street four years. On the 2nd of June, about half-past eleven o'clock at night, I was going up Old Nicholl-street, and saw Griffin come out of Masonplace and go to Shepherd's-court, with a bundle on her arm—I went to the corner of Maidstone-court and saw Cole, and another man with him, about twenty yards off Griffin-Cole had something white across his arm—I supposed it was sheets—it was white like cloth—they went to the left—the water-closet is right opposite as you go up the court—I stopped at the bottom of the court—I went with Sarah Cowles to give information at the station—a policeman came, and saw the things taken out of the water-closet.
THOMAS HUGHES . I live in Vine-court, Spitalfields. On the 2nd of June, about twelve o'clock, I went to No. 1, Maidstone-place, I found the door broken open, a padlock lying inside, and the bed stripped—I afterwards went to Shepherd's-court, and found part of the property in the water-closet—I saw Griffin crouching behind the door of No. 4, and Cole standing at the corner of the court, a few yards from her—I went in search of another man, who was suspected, came back, found Griffin in custody, went to her room, and found the remainder of the property on the bed—this is it.
LOUISA COWLES re-examined. These are my mother's property—they were let with the room.
JOHN HOPE ( policeman.) On the 2nd of June, between eleven and twelve o'clock, I was passing up Old Nicholl-street, and saw Mr. Cowes, Griffin, and a person named Mills, who told me something—the prisoner Cole was not present—I went into the room, and found the bed stripped—I saw an apron at the head of the bed—it was handed to me by Mills—I went in search of the prisoner Cole, and found him at the corner of Church-street, by a public-house—I told him I wanted him, and he followed us to the station.
Cole's Defence. I was not there, and know nothing of the robbery.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1505]
1505. JOHN WILLIAMS was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Johns, at Christchurch, and stealing 1 bolster, value 4 s.; 1 blanket, 5 s.; and 3 ornaments, 6 d., his goods; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES JOHNS . I live at No. 6, Wilk-street, Spitalfields. On the 28th of May, about eleven o'clock at night, I went to bed—I was the last person up—I saw the house all safe—about half-past four o'clock next morning I got up to work—a neighbour tapped at my staircase window—I went down, and found the street-door wide open—it had
been close shut, when I went to bed—I went into the back parlour—the window was wide open—it had been close shut when I went to bed—I missed a bolster, blanket, two dresses, and a great coat, which have never been found—I went into the street with Mr. Theroulde, and saw the prisoner come out of the next house—he had three ornaments with him, which had been on my mantelpiece when I fastened up on Friday night—I went after him, and cried, "Stop thief"—he ran the whole length of the street into a house, and hid himself behind the street-door—the policeman there picked up these ornaments—they are my son's, James Johns—he lives in the house with me—the prisoner was taken to the station—this blanket and bolster are my property, and were safe in the room before I went to bed.
CHARLOTTE THEROULDE . I live at No. 5, Wilk-street, next door to Mr. Johns. On Saturday morning, the 29th of May, about half-past four o'clock, I and my husband got up—I slept in the back parlour—I went into the back room, and saw Mr. Johns' back window open—I took no notice of it, as the weather was warm—I went to my own back window on the third floor, looked out, and saw the prisoner jump out of Mr. Johns' window, with the blanket under his arm—my little boy was standing at the back door—the prisoner said to him, "Hallo, Jack!"—I did not know the prisoner before—he jumped over the wall of the wash-house to Mr. Johns'—I tapped at Mr. Johns' window, gave an alarm, and afterwards saw the prisoner in the street and Mr. Johns after him—I am sure the prisoner is the person—I did not go to the house, nor run after him—I saw the pillow and the blanket laying at the foot of the stairs—I took them up—the policeman came, and took them from me.
SAMUEL THROULDE . I live with my father and mother. On the 29th of May, I got up at the same time as them, looked out at the window, and saw the prisoner walking up and down by the door—I dressed myself, went down, stood at the back door, and then saw him come from the back-parlour window, with the bolster and blanket under his arm—he was getting over the wall, turned his head, saw me, and said, "Hallo, Jack!"—I am sure he is the man that came out of the window.
WILLIAM WALKON ( Policeman.) About half-past four or near five o'clock I was on duty in Wilk-street, heard a cry of "Stop thief," saw the prisoner running, went after him, and caught him—he went into a house about a hundred yards from Mr. Johns'—I looked in, and found him concealed behind the door, I took him into custody, and found three images on the spot—these are them.
WILLIAM WATSON . On the morning of the 29th of May, I received this blanket and bolster from Mr. Theroulde—the house is in the parish of Christchurch, Spitalfields.
Prisoner's Defence. I was taken bad in my bowels, saw the door open, and went in; when I came out into the street, the blanket and bolster were lying in the entry.
JOHN BARKER ( police-constable K 22.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted Dec., 1845, and confined four months)—I was present at the trial—he is the person.
GUILTY .* * Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18470614-1506]
1506. JOHN LEAHY was indicted for feloniously assaulting Thomas Holmes, and cutting and wounding him on the left cheek, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
THOMAS HOLMES . I am a porter, and live at 5, Cooper's-court, Knights-bridge.
On the 8th of June, about half-past one o'clock in the morning, I was going home, and saw some men quarrelling and fighting—I did not go up to them—I only walked by them—the people themselves were throwing stones at one another—the prisoner was seven or eight yards from me—I am quite positive it was him—as I passed he threw a stone at me, which knocked me down—he then ran up and kicked me—he did not come in front of me, but sideways—the stone hit me on the lower jaw, knocked me down, and knocked one of my teeth out—when I fell, he ran up and kicked me on the side of my face—I got up and ran after him—he ran away, and fell down—I overtook him—the policeman came up, and I gave him in charge—I went to the station-house with him, and from there to St. George's Hospital—I am quite certain he is the man—I never lost sight of him.
Cross-examined by MR. BALDWIN. Q. Were you sober? A. Perfectly—I had just left work—It was between Monday and Tuesday, in the night—there were about a dozen people, and there were a general scuffle and row —the prisoner was standing at the corner of a cook's shop with a stone in his hand, not near the other people—they were in the high road—he was on the same side of me as the other people—I saw him throw the stone, and felt it strike me—I was passing in the middle of the road when he threw the stone—it is a wide open place—I had never seen him before.
MARY ANN COX . I am the wife of John Cox, of Sonw's-rents, Westminster On the 8th of June, about half-past one o'clock in the morning, I was on Knightsbridge-green, and saw the prisoner with a stone in his hand—when Holmes was passing by, he threw the stone at him, hit him in the mouth, knocked him down, and then ran and kicked him—Holmes ran after him and caught him.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you been there long? A. About ten minutes before the row occurred—I saw the prisoner stoop and pick up the stone upon Knightsbridge-green—there were a lot of people throwing stones—none of them were near him when he took it—they were out in the road—he threw the stone not above two or three minutes after he took it up—I called "Police" as loud as I could.
THOMAS RANDALL ( police-constable B 231.) About one o'clock in the morning I saw two men running on Knightsbridge-green—I heard a cry of "Police," went up, and found Holmes and the prisoner together—Holmes gave the prisoner in charge.
JOHN MACHIN . I am house-surgeon at St. George's Hospital—Holmes was brought there on the 8th of June—I examined him—he had a fracture of the lower jaw, a severe wound on the inner side of the cheek, and a severe bruise, which might have been caused by a kick or a stone, or both.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1507]
1507. MORRIS FREYER was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of May, at St. Botolph Without, Bishopsgate, 1 bag, value 2 s.; 1 box, 1 l.; 4 s.; 65 watches, 130 l.; 7 breast-pins, 10 l.; 4 brooches, 5 l.; 36 spoons, 9 l.; 3 seals, 2 l.; 6 rings, 20 l.; 1 1/2 oz. pearls, 25 l.; the goods of Kirsch Landsberg, in the dwelling-house of Charlotte Parish.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
KIRSCH LANDSBERG . I am a Pole, and deal in jewellery. On the 25th of May I lived at No. 5, New-street, Bishopsgate, kept by Mr. Parish—the
prisoner occupied a third floor in the same house—he was something in the same line of business as myself. On the 25th of May I was Playing at cards with him and another man, who had come up from Maidstone, and who used to lodge on some other floor—I had brought home a gold watch, at five o'clock—I showed that and some other things to the prisoner—the other man came in while we were playing—they saw me put the things into a box—I locked it, and put it into a blue bag—it was a square box—I used to carry it in the bag—the other man did not see the contents of the box—me all three went out—when we were standing in front of the street door, they asked me which way I was going—I said to Bishopsgate—they then went the contrary way—I returned about nine o'clock—I had left my box locked—I had not left my door locked, because there is no key—I missed the box and contents, and have never seen them since—I gave information to the landlady—the prisoner came home at eleven o'clock, and I gave him in charge—he had no right to go into my room when I was out—I had lived there about six months—the room door goes rather hard, and makes a great deal of noise.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALF. Q. Have not you been occasionally in the prison's room, and he in yours? A. I was never in his room—he used not to come into my room till within a few days before this, but he had come nearly very day for the last eight days—I always opened the box, and put in the goods, when I came home—we had only played at cards two or three times, and not more then twice in the last eight days, and he used to come to my room perhaps twice a day during the last eight days—I made no objection—I did not like to—I thought it very curious—the other man's name is Cohen—he is a foreigner—I have known him ten years—I know nothing about him—he has not returned since—he had come up from Maidstone the previous day—I gave the prisoner in charge, about eleven o'clock, in the parlour down stair—there are two or three lodgers, most of them in the same trade as myself—I never saw the prisoner with jewellery—I have never been up to his room—I have no other name but Kirsch Landsberg—when I saw the prisoner I did not speak to him, but gave him in charge.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Had you previously had some conversation with Mr. Parish? A. Yes—that led me to give him in charge—Cohen came home before him—I do not know how long before, as I was not at home.
EMMA CHARLOTTE PARISH . My mother is a window, and keeps the house No. 5, New-street, Bishopsgate, in the parish of St. Botolph Without, Bishopsgate; Landsberg lodged in the first floor front room, Cohen at the back, and the prisoner on the third floor. On the 25th of May, about six o'clock, the prosecutor, the prisoner, and Cohen went out together—after they had been gone about five minutes the prisoner returned—I opened the door—he went into the parlour—I went in, then left him there, and went down stairs—he then went up stairs and opened Mr. Landsberg's room—the door makes a noise when it is opened; it bursts—when I heard the door I spoke to my mother, and she went up stairs—there was no other person in the house but a lady on the second floor—my mother was up stairs three or four minutes—the prisoner then went out—he turned to the left, and I could not see him as I looked to the right—I heard somebody go out—the lady was not out all that day—there was one gentleman lived in the house—he came in twice, and went out twice—I let him in once, and let him out once, and my mother let him in once, and out once—he had got nothing with him—another gentleman came once, and let himself in—he had nothing with him, and I let him out—neither of them could have had a bag or box without my seeing it.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you first see Cohen? A. When he came home, about eleven o'clock—he went out about six—we sometimes have a great many lodgers—at this time there were three besides the prisoner and Cohen—I am quite sure neither of them were in the house between six and nine, except when I let them out twice—one was in the house all the evening, with the exception of half an hour, and the other one, Mr. Landsberg went out for a few minutes—I let him out—he told me he went to meet Mr. Greenberg—he staid out about half an hour—he returned about seven—the other one came in about a quarter to seven, went out at half-past seven, was out about a quarter of an hour, and then did not go out again—Greenberg lived in the same room with the prosecutor—the other man lived up stairs, on the third floor—I heard the prisoner go to the door of Landsberg's room—I did not hear it shut again—it does not make so much noise in shutting—I did not go up till Mr. Landsberg returned, as I had no idea of anything wrong—when I first went up stairs the door was open, but Mr. Landsberg had been up first, and I went up with him again—Rose-alley is round the corner of our street—the prisoner staid about five minutes in the house—I cannot fix the time exactly.
CHARLOTTE PARISH . I keep this house. On the 25th of May my daughter said something to me—I let in the prisoner—I heard him go into the first floor front room—he had no business there whatever—I said something to my daughter, then went up into the front parlour, and heard him, through the ceiling, walking across the room—I did not go up—there was nobody in the house but him and a lady on the first floor—I heard him come out in about five minutes—I had not seen the box or bag—about nine o'clock the prosecutor told me what had occurred—I said I had not been in the room during the time—other persons came in and out, I let them in and out; they had not anything with them—the prisoner had nothing with him—he could not have had the box or bag concealed, without my seeing it.
Cross-examined. Q. Is there a latch-key hanging up behind your door? A. No; there is a large key which the prisoner might have taken with him if he chose, but should have gone to the door instantly if I had heard the key put in, as it is not usual to take it.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Did you hear it put in? A. No; I did not miss it.
JOHN SULLIVAN . I live with my father and mother, at No. 7, Rose-alley, Bishopsgate. On the 25th of May I saw the prisoner coming from Mr. Parish's house—he was about twice the length of this court from the door—he turned to the left—I am certain he is the man—he had a blur bag of the common size with him, which had four corners to it, and seemed to be knocking against his knees and thighs—I am sure there was something in it—it was like a box which came up slanting—there was another man with him who held the bag while the prisoner stood opposite the door and made water—I saw them both come out together from Mr. Landsberg's house—while the prisoner made water the other man walked round the corner, to see if any one was coming—they looked frightened—the man with the bag kept staring about—the prisoner took off his hat and put on a cap—I followed them—they ran down the court as fast as they could—I saw no more of them—I next saw the prisoner in the Compter—there were about twelve or thirteen men there—a gentleman said, if I knew the man I was to pick him out—I walked round and laid my finger on him—it was about half-past six o'clock in the evening—my father works in the St. Katharine Docks.
Cross-examined. Q. Where were you standing? A. At the top of
Rose-alley, that is at the corner of New-street—they both came round the further corner of New-street together, at the other end, by the public-house—they walked part of the way up New-street, came down facing the door, and made water—New-street leads into Cock-hill—when I first saw them they were about twice the length of this Count from the house—Cock-hill is a continuation of New-street, but there is an angle in it—the other man was a thin-featured man, with his hat turned up, and a coat with a round top to it—I had never seen him or the prisoner before—he stopped to make water, half-a-minute's walk from the house—I am sure it was a blue bag that the prisoner had—when they made water I stared at them—I wondered what they were frightened at—they looked round to see if they could see anybody, but did not take any notice of me.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Was he near the public-house when he made water? A. No; he came from the public-house—I could see him distinctly—they ran down the court where my father lived, which leads into Bishopsgate-street—I saw no more of them.
THOMAS FOLLETT . I am a porter, and live at No. 12, West-street, Sobo. I was lately taken before a Magistrate, and charged—while I was in the Compter the prisoner was there—I had a conversation with him—he asked me what I was there for—I told him what I was there for—he said he was accused of stealing a box of jewels—he said there was a boy there whose evidence was more in his favour than against him—he said the box was worth 200 l., but to break them up they were not worth 100 l.—he said he had often played at cards with the prosecutor, had seen the contents of the box, and was very sweet on them—he expected to be discharged on the same day I was; and said if he was discharged he would try the weight of the box—there had been no other charge about any other box—he referred to this box as far as I understood.
JOHN SPITAL , ( policeman.) On the night of the 25th of May I took the prisoner into custody—I asked him whether he had been to Mr. Landsberg's room—he said "No"—I said, "Why did you return so soon to the room?"—he said, "If you have any charge against me, I shall not say anything.
GUILTY. Aged 26,— Judgment respited.
OLD COURT, Tuesday, June 22 nd, 1847.
PRESENT —Mr. Alderman HUNTER; and Mr. Common Sergeant.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
