1508. HENRY FARR was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, at St. Leonard, Shoreditch, 1 cash-box, value 2 s.; 12 sovereigns, 16 half-sovereigns, 3 crowns, 17 half-crowns, 80 shillings, 40 sixpences, 40 groats, 1 double sovereign, 1 piece of foreign coin, 6 d.; an order for the payment and value of 20 l.; 1 other order for 1 l. 11 s. 6 d.; 1 for 12 l.; a bill of exchange for 100 l.; 1 for 52 l.; 1 for 30 l.; a valuable security for the payment of 24 l.; 1 for 16 l.; and 1 for 11 l.; 1 20 l.; 2 10 l.; and 2 5 l. bank-notes; and 3 pieces of stamped paper, 7 s. 6 d. each; the property of William John Bird, his master, in his dwelling-house; and CHARLES FARR as an accessory after the fact, and for feloniously receiving the said property, well knowing it to have been stolen.
WILLIAM JOHN BIRD . I keep the Mail Coach public-house in Kingsland-road—the
prisoner, Henry Farr, was in my service as pot-boy for about three weeks—on the afternoon of Easter Sunday my house was closed, and nobody but myself, the barmaid, and Henry Farr were in the house—a friend of the servant girl's was there—I had a cash-box in my beaufet, which was in my bar-parlour—it was not locked—it contained a 20 l. Bank of England note, 2 10 l. notes, 2 or 3 fives, 20 l. in gold, and some silver—three was a 6 d. and a 4 d. piece among it which were remarkable, the 6 d. had a hole in it—there was also a double sovereign—I missed that cash-box about a quarter to four o'clock—I had seen it safe at three o'clock—I went up stairs, and immediately I came down I went into the yard and found the back gate open—my suspicions were directly excited—I looked for Henry Farr, and he had gone out without his hat or cap, and left his clothes behind him—I went in pursuit of him—I had seen Charles Farr once—I traced Henry to his mother's, but did not see him there—I went down to Bridgenorth on the 5th of May, and there saw Charles Farr, and on the 7th of May I found him in custody at Hoxton—I said, "I have got you at last," or something to that effect—he said, "Oh, you don't want me; it was not me that took the cash-box, it was my brother, and you did not see him take it"—he taunted me with that in the station-house—he was searched, and 3 l. some silver found on him, among which was a 6 d. with a hole in it on one side—this now produced is it—I will undertake to swear that it is the one I lost—I said to him at the time, "This sixpence was in the cash-box"—(it was along with a 4 d. piece and a half-crown that was battered—there were several remarkable things)—he said it was not—I know it by the hole and by the general appearance of it—it has been through my hands repeatedly—it is knocked up at the side—I also said to him, "Why, you are dressed like a gentleman now, and you had no clothes before, where have you got all these from? it is my money that has bought these"—he said, "You cannot prove it," or something to that effect—on the same night, I saw Henry Farr at Bow-street, and charged him with stealing my cash-box—he said, "I did not do it," or something to that effect.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How long had you this sixpence? A. I dare say three weeks or a month.
JAMES ARIEL . I keep the Rent-day public-house in Cambridge-street, Paddington—Charles Farr lived in my service—on Easter Sunday last he left me about a quarter to six, and did not return—he left all his clothes behind him, and I owed him wages—I know nothing of Henry Farr.
LUCY BIRD . I am the prosecutor's wife—on Friday, the 7th of May, I was in Searle's-place, Temple-bar, and saw Charles Farr going into a house there in which his mother lodges—I waited outside some time, and at last he came out—he walked very fast, holding his head down—he might have seen me—I was in a position for him to see me—I kept up with him—he went round Ship-yard into the Strand, then came again through Temple-bar to the bottom of Searle's-place—he went up to a cab, spoke to the cab man, and said, "Drive on"—he was in the act of getting into the cab when I saw Henry Farr inside the cab —I put my hand through the cab window and got hold of Henry Farr, upon which Charles retired back from the cab, and shut the door, shutting my arm in the window—it grazed my arm dreadfully, and caused me to let go—Henry then made a rush at the opposite window and escaped from the other door—I eaught hold of Charles—he said, "You don't know me"—I said, "I do"—he immediately said, "You have no charge against me, it is my brother you want"—I said, "You have been both
together"—he said, "I have not robbed you; my brother took the cash-box, I did not do it, and you did not see him do it"—he objected to my giving him into custody, and tried to get away—a policeman came up and I gave him into custody—I saw him searched, and afterwards saw the sixpence—I know it to be the sixpence that we had—I swear to it
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear Charles Farr say where he had got the sixpence from? A. No, I did not.
GEORGE SMITH ( City-police-constable, No. 337.) I heard some disturbance and went to see what was the matter—I saw Charles Farr by the side of the cab, and Mr. Bird holding him—she charged him with robbing her—he said he was not the one she wanted—I saw some clothes in the cab which I produce, also a carpet bag and two bundles—I asked Charles Farr about them—he said, "They are not mine, they are my brother's"—I took him to the station and found 3 l. 10 s. on him, this sixpence was among it—I found this watch and a silver guard-chain on him—the guard-chain appears to be new, but nothing else—I do not think the clothes are new—he was dressed in new clothes.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear Charles Farr say he had got the sixpence in change in the country? A. No.
WILLIAM FRIEND . I keep an Alton ale-house in Chancery-lane—about nine o'clock on Friday evening the 7th of May, as I was sitting in my parlour, Henry Farr came in and asked for some ale—my wife was serving him—he said he was a young man from the country, and asked her to take charge of three sovereigns until the following morning, when he would call for it—she called me out, I went out, and he asked me the same question—I consented to take charge of the three sovereigns—he said he was going out for a spree and did not want to have more money about him than he thought he should want to spend—he counted over the money he had in his hand, turned round again, and said, "Perhaps you will take charge of these two half-crowns as well"—he said he would call for the money in the morning—he did not call, as he was then in custody.
JAMES DOWSETT ( police-constable F 136.) On the 7th of May I was on the look out for Henry Farr—I saw him pass me—I stopped him, and told him I took him in charge for stealing a cash-box—directly I laid hold of him he said, "Anything you like to have, you may have to drink"—I said I was not thirsty—he asked me to let him go and speak to his mother—I took him to the station, searched him, and found on him some half-sovereigns and other money—he was dressed in a good suit of black clothes, which appeared to have been recently purchased.
HENRY FARR — GUILTY . Aged 18.
CHARLES FARR — GUILTY . Aged 17.
Judgment Respited.
( Mr. Robinson stated that there was reason to believe the lost money was still under the prisoners' control.)
[trialAccount t18470614-1510]
1510. THOMAS TRUMAN was indicted for embezzling the sums of 2 l. 2 s. 8 d., 1 l. 6 s. 8 d., and 2 l. 6 s. 8 d., which he had received by virtue of his employment as clerk to and on account of Martin Goodwin and others, his masters: other COUNTS, stating him to be employed as servant, and in the capacity of clerk to the said Martin Goodwin and others: and other COUNTS, charging a larceny as servant, and simple larceny.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM ROBERTS JAMES . I am clerk to the Board of Guardians of the Holborn Union, and vestry-clerk of the liberty of Saffron-hill, which is part of the Holborn Union—the prisoner was a collector of the poors' rates—he was appointed on the 1st of June, 1836.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Was that appointment in writing? A. No—it was not an appointment in writing under the hand and seal of the Poor Law Commissioners—it is according t practice that the collector is appointed by order of the Poor Law Commissioners—that is a printed order—there was a printed order in this case.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Not appointing the collector? A. No, it is an order to the Guardians to appoint, which is done at a meeting of the Board—he is appointed verbally—a minute of his appointment is afterwards made in the minute-book.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Is the appointment made by virtue of the order of the Poor Law Commissioners? A. Certainly, and that is a printed order, describing his duties and the payment he is to receive—the entry of his appointment in the minute-book is made the next day.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Have you got the rate-book here? A. I have—the rate to which the amounts in question refer was made on the 13th of Aug., 1846—I know the handwriting of the gentlemen whose names appear in the book—I have seen them write—it was the prisoner's duty to collect that rate, and when collected to pay it into the bankers to the account of the Overseers—I know that the Overseers have an account at the bankers—the audit up to Lady-day, 1847, commenced on the 4th of May—Mr. James Hales Mitchener is the auditor—about the 6th of May the prisoner was sent for by the auditor to bring his receipt check-books—he did not bring them—they were brought or sent some days after, but I was not present—the prisoner was present when the accounts were audited—the auditor expressed an opinion that explanations were necessary and requested him to produce certain books—no amount of defalcation was then stated to him—it was not correctly ascertained at that time what the amount was—he was sent to get his receipt check-books, and was told to return immediately—I did not see him for several days afterwards—I produce the check-books, and also a return that was made by the prisoner to the auditor on the 6th of May—there are two accounts; one form Lady-day to Midsummer, 1846, and the other from Midsummer to Michaelmas, 1846—the greater part of this is in the prisoner's handwriting—he produced it, and that which is not his handwriting are some observations of the auditor's—the result of the account from Michaelmas to Christmas is, that there was a balance in his hand of 24 l. 14 s. 6 d.—the amount that was uncollected was 446 l. 15 s. 1 d.—that was the deficiency—this is only an aggregate account—I have no detailed account of prisoner's of the particular sums paid to him, and not accounted for by him—the auditor has a detailed account, made out by the prisoner and himself, going through the book, and putting down the sums—the prisoner was paid by a poundage of 2 l. 10 s. per cent. on the receipt of rates—he was paid by the Overseers—that has continued since his appointment—I have the Overseers' account book.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Have you the order of the Poor Law Commissioners appointing this man? A. I have the order, directing the Guardians to proceed to the election—it is a printed order, under the seal of the Commissioners—upon that, the Guardians proceeded to appoint the prisoner—that appointment was made at a meeting, and a minute of it
afterwards entered in the book—information was given to the Poor Law Commissioners of his appointment—that was in writing.
Q. Was there then a ratification of his appointment sent by the Poor-law Commissioners, prescribing what his duties were? A. No, merely approving his appointment—his duties are prescribed in the order—the Guardians are bound by that prescription of the duties—the order directing the election, also prescribes the duties—the prisoner was appointed by the Guardians—he has since been dismissed by the Poor-law Commissioners, not by the Guardians, they have no power of dismissal—the prisoner gave a bond to the Guardians on being appointed—I have got that bond—every Wednesday morning he was expected to show to the Guardians how the rates stood—he did not go before the Guardians, he rendered an account every Wednesday morning to me, as clerk to the Guardians, of the money he had received on the different rates that were then due—it can hardly be called a weekly account—it was the result of the monies collected—the Guardians are the Guardians of the Union—they direct a certain sum of money to be paid by the Overseers to them, as their share of the expenses of the Union—the money is collected for the Overseers, and paid to the account of the Overseers, at the banker's—they have to pay a great many small local expenses, Police-rates, and so on, and then they pay the amount ordered by the Guardians over to the Guardians.
COURT. Q. Are the sums he is charged with embezzling, included in the sum of which you have been speaking? A. No.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Is Mr. Mitchener the auditor of the district, or of the whole Union? A. Of the district—he is the auditor for that district, as well as for other places—the prisoner is paid his commission by the Overseers.
COURT. Q. Do you mean that he is paid out of the rate of the parish? A. No, paid by the Overseers, by the hand of the Overseers—there is no deduction.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Look at that receipt, is that the prisoner's handwriting? A. It is, ( reading it,) "Received of the Overseers of the Liberty of Saffron-hill, the sum of 42 l. 2 s. 9 d., for poundage to Michaelmas last. Thomas Truman"—he is paid by a poundage—the money collected is paid in to the bankers, to the account of the Overseers, the Overseers have to pay the Police-rate, the County-rate, &c., that is without any connection with the Guardians, quite independent of them—the Guardians know nothing of it, in fact all that they know is with regard to their own receipt for the maintainance and support of the poor—the Overseers pay a certain amount to the treasurer of the Union, for the Guardians for the relief of the poor, and the surplus, after that amount is so paid, is retained by the Overseers for the next year.
Q. There is no accounting, as I understand, by the Overseers to the Guardians at all? A. There is the result of every Wednesday—the collector is expected to show the balance of each rate as it stands at that time.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. To whom? A. To me, as the clerk to the Guardians, the balance of the different rates that are then in collection, so that if there is any laxity on the part of the collector, the Guardians might communicate with the Overseers.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. The Guardians meet every Wednesday? A. They do, twice, at ten in the morning and seven in the evening—the Overseers have no regular time of meeting—if it was necessary to show the account to the Overseers, it would be requisite to call a special meeting if them for the purpose, or to communicate in writing—that is the mode generally adopted——he
does account to the Overseers—he pays the money in to the account of the Overseers.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Have you anything to do with that? A. I am vestry-clerk to the district, but I do not interfere in that account—I only interfere with the business of the Overseers, so far as regards the making of the rate.
Q. Where did you ever see him account to the Overseers? A. That book is rendered.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Is there any other account rendered than that book? A. The weekly account—I am not aware that there is any account rendered to the Overseers, except that which is rendered to the auditor.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. That is the auditor's book, is it not? A. No, it is the Overseer' book—it is the book which the auditor refers to.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. You say he accounts to the Overseers? A. Certainly—he pays the money into the banker's, and he produces the banker's book as the Overseers' book, to show the payments made by him on account of the Overseer, to the banker's—he accounts to the Overseers quarterly, at all events the accounts are made up for the purposes of the auditor—this is the quarterly-book—it is made up quarterly for the purpose of the auditor making up the accounts—I have got the Guardians weekly returns—these entries are made by the prisoner—I do not know anything of their being made, except that the book is in his custody, and is produced by the prisoner at the time of the audit.
COURT. Q. Who are Martin Goodwin, and others? A. the Overseers.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Is that one of the orders that he returns to the Guardians?—( producing a paper). A. It is one of the returns—it is in his handwriting, and has his signature—at the bottom of this paper the following words are printed, "That the collector do make a correct return to the Board of Guardians, in the above improved form; and that a copy thereof be also delivered to Overseers weekly."
ADAM HAMILTON . I am a rate-payer, and live at No. 73, Hatton-garden, within the Liberty of Saffron-hill. On the 27th of Aug. I paid the prisoner 2 l. 2 s. 8 d. for a poors'-rate, from Midsummer to Michaelmas, 1846—I produce the prisoner's receipt.
ISAAC SIMPSON . I am a rate-payer, and reside at No. 13, Charles-street, within the Liberty of Saffron-hill. I paid the prisoner 1 l. 6 s. 8 d. on the 27th of Aug.—I saw him write the receipt, which I produce—it is for one quarter's poors-rate, from Midsummer to Michaelmas, 1846.
MR. BAKER. I live at No. 56, Hatton-garden. I paid prisoner 2 l. 6 s. 8 d. on the 13th of Oct., 1846, for which he gave me the receipt I now produce—it is for one quarter's poors'-rate, from Midsummer to michaelmas, 1846.
MR. JAMES ( re-examined.) These are sums that were collected by the prisoner under the rate to which I have already spoken.
JAMES HALES MITCHINER . I am the auditor of the district in which the Holborn Union is situated. I audit the accounts of each Union separately—Saffron-hill is a distinct district—the Union consists of a number of parishes or districts, and I audit the accounts of each distinctly—I am elected by the Guardians of the districts, and the Poor-law Commissioners ratify the appointment—I audit half-yearly now—the prisoner has been in the habit of producing his books, and accounting to me for the district of Saffron-hill—on Thursday morning, the 6th of May, I proceeded to audit those accounts—he produced this account-book to me—it is an aggregate account—on looking
over the book, there were some circumstances which excited my suspicion, and I sent the prisoner home for his check-books—these are receipt check-books—he takes these out with him—when he receives money he tears out a receipt, gives it to the person paying the money, and enters in the margin the person's name from whom he receives it, and the amount and date—when I sent him for his check-books, I expected him back immediately—he did not return—I did not see him again until Saturday morning, the 8th of May, when he came to me in consequence of some communication I had had with his friends—I then went into a detailed examination in his presence of the check-books with the rate-books—in the first place I took an account of those rates which appeared by the check-books to be unpaid—the receipt-books were produced—there are receipts in them for the money of Mr. Hamilton, Mr. Baker, and Mr. Simpson, and he stated at the time that they were unpaid by them—I asked him the question upon each particular item—the fact of the receipts being here, would purport that they were not paid, but not in all cases, because in some cases he gives stamp receipts—that was why I asked him upon each particular item—the three receipts which have been produced, are not stamp-receipts, but similar to those in the book—he denied having received these—as far as I know, this book is in the same condition as it was when I received it, except the writing that is across the receipts.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe the course is this, after the rate is made the collector collects as much as he can of it? A. Yes, and I believe he reports to the Guardians those persons who are defaulters, and the reasons of their default—the rate-books are not actually closed until the cases are sent before the Magistrate—neither of the rates in which these sums are deficient are closed.
COURT. Q. What do you mean by not being closed? A. The rate-book is not balanced up, showing the amount to be recovered—there are still sums to be received upon it—that is the case every audit—it is not exactly carried into the next rate—the particulars of each house that is empty or recoverable is shown in the rate-book.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. How many hundred receipts did you go through at a time with the prisoner? A. I went through the whole of them—there are some hundreds—they laid before me, and I compared the items with the rate-book as I went on—as I came to each case in which the receipt appeared, I read out the name, and to each he said, "That is not paid," with two or three exceptions, where he stated he had given stamp-receipts, there being two or three assessments to one man—he was sitting by my side, looking over the books—I do not know that it often happens that the collector has a rate paid when he has not his rate-book in his pocket, and that he gives a receipt from some other rate-book—it is a very improper mode if he does so.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Just look over the receipt-book, and tell me the amount which he said had not been received, but which you have since found he has in fact received? A. About 69 l., that is not in this book only—the receipt that should have been given to Mr. Hamilton is still in this book—I presume he must have given one of the same kind to Mr. Hamilton, when he received the money—he ought to have the receipts written out before he goes out to collect.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Do you know, on one occasion, of his returning about 40 l. as paid, which turned out afterwards to be unpaid to him? A. No;—a certain sum was declared as the amount of the prisoner's deficiency—I have heard that his goods were sold, but I have no personal knowledge of it—it has not come officially before me—I heard him say that the reason of his
being deficient was, that a bill in Chancery had been preferred against him for some duties that he had performed as an executor.
MR. HUDDLESTION to MR. JAMES. Q. Do you know of the prisoner's paying about 40 l. into the bank on this very occasion, which turned out not to have been paid to him? A. I do not know that he did, I never heard of it—he handed over to me a pare I of old receipts, some upwards of ten years old, which he stated he had paid for the parties, but had not received the money from them; but they are of no value—I do not know that there was 40 l. unpaid to him on this very account—I do not know that there was any—he did not hand me those old receipts by the wish of the Guardians—I have them here.
GUILTY.— Judgment respited.
NEW COURT.— Tuesday, June 22, 1847.
PRESENT —Mr. Alderman MOON; and EDWARD BULLOCK, Esq.
Sixth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18470614-1511]
1511. JOHN CHARLISH was indicted for feloniously cutting and wounding Edward Jarvis on the head, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. BALDWIN conducted the Prosecution.
EDWARD JARVIS . I am a smith and farrier, in the employ of Mr. Jones, in Great Leonard-street, Shoreditch. The prisoner worked there for about fourteen months—he left on the Saturday evening before Easter Sunday—he came to the shop on Monday, the 31st of May, about half-past six o'clock in the evening—I was working there—he had left some tools there—he came to fetch them away—I think he had some beer—he was there a quarter of an hour before I spoke to him—I was at work at the forge making a shoe for a gentleman—I was half an hour about it, and he kept on blackguarding me—I told him to be quite and hold his tongue—I had done that shoe and then I went to do another little job—he came up to me and, "Jarvis, you are a d—d thief, and a rogue"—I said, "If you call me that again, I will give you in charge"—I pushed him from me, and told him to go away, and come when Mr. Jones was at home—at that time he had got his tools in his apron, and he said, "You are Mr. Jones's foreman, look at what tools I have got"—I looked, and saw a hammer which did not belong to the prisoner—it had been there two years before he-came—he had put a handle in this hammer, and was going to take it away—I told him it was not his—he said, "If it is Mr. Jones's hammer, it is my handle, and I will saw it off"—some time after I went to put my hand to him to take the hammer, and I said, "You go away"—he said, "I will go when I like"—I went to put my hand to it and he took up the hammer and hit me twice on the head with it—I bled a great deal, and was taken to the surgeon—I was in bed from Monday till Thursday, and did not leave my room till Saturday—I had not struck the prisoner before I received those blows.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. The prisoner was a little in drink when he came? A. I do not know whether he was—I told him he was—I thought he was, by his appearance—I was thinking he was, but he told me he knew his own business—he did not speak to me for a quarter of an hour—he stood in the shop and spoke to the other men—the first thing he said to me was, that he was going to take his tools—I told him afterwards that I should fetch a policeman
if he did not go out—he said I might give him in charge if I liked, and I said I did not want to do anything of the sort if he went out quietly—he told me he would give me in charge if I hit him, but I did not—I did not say to him, "Will you pull me if I hit you?"—I did not put my fist in his face, nor touch him at all—I did not pull his nose, nor threaten to pull it—I did not lay hold of the hammer that I know of—I tried to take it away—I might put my hand on it—I told him he should not take the hammer away—I put my hand on his shoulder and pushed him away—I told him he should not take the hammer without Mr. Jones was at home—I neither put my fist in his face, nor pulled his nose, nor threatened to pull it.
JURY. Q. Did the prisoner work piece-work, or day-work? A. Our work is all piece-work—the prisoner had done something to the hammer.
JOHN DEAN . I work at Mr. Jones's—I remember on the evening of the 31st May, the prisoner coming to the shop—Jarvis was there—I left the shop for about ten minutes, while the prisoner was there—when I came back Jarvis and the prisoner were wrangling—the prisoner was abusing Jarvis—I told him he had better come in the morning, when Mr. Jones was at home—I was about to leave the shop, I heard a noise, turned round and saw the prisoner strike Jarvis twice with the hammer—a man took the hammer from him.
Cross-examined. Q. Who separated them after the blow was struck? A. I cannot say—a man named Wren, was there—I heard Jarvis say, "Let me get at him"—I was not there during the whole time—I did not hear Jarvis threaten to pull his nose—I did not see him put his fist in his face—when I saw this scuffle, the hammer was up in the air—Jarvis had not got his hands on the prisoner's arm—I do not know where his hands were.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Jarvis said, "Let me get at him?" A. Yes—that was after the blows were struck.
ANN BEASLEY . I am in Mr. Jones's service. On the evening of the 31st of May, I was on the stairs which look into the shop—the prisoner came for his tools—he had been there some time—I heard him ask Jarvis to look at his tools—Jarvis said he did not want anything to do with him, to go out of the shop—after he laid his tools down on his apron in the shop, Jarvis saw a hammer, he said it was his master's, and the prisoner should not take it away—the prisoner called Jarvis a d—d thief and a rouge—Jarvis told him to go out of the shop, he did not want anything to say to him—the prisoner came round to Jarvis, while Jarvis was at work by the fire, Jarvis turned round and said if he did not go he would pull his nose—he put his hand to his face, I did not see whether he pulled his nose—they were quarrelling for some time—I did not hear what they said—they walked down the shop as if they were going out of the gate—Jarvis put his hand on the prisoner's shoulder, and said he would take the hammer from him, he should not take it out of the shop—the prisoner turned round and struck him twice on the head with the hammer—I was frightened, and went away.
Cross-examined. Q. It was when Jarvis told the prisoner he should not take the hammer away that the prisoner called him a rouge and a thief, was it not? A. Yes—after that, Jarvis told him if he did not go away he would pull his nose, and he put his fist in his face—I heard the prisoner say to him, "If you strike me I will have you up"—before these blows were struck, Jarvis put his hand on the prisoner's shoulder, and put his other hand before the prisoner, as though he were going to take the hammer from him—the prisoner swung round, and then I saw the hammer fall down on his head.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Do you mean that it fell accidentally, or were they blows struck? Q. They were blows struck.
THOMAS BOYLAN . I work at Mr. Jones. On the evening of the 31st of May, I was at the shop—the prisoner came for his tools—he fetched some of them, and put them in his apron—he asked for his hammer, and got hold of it—Jarvis said the hammer did not belong to him—he said it did, and they began to quarrel, and called one another rogues and thieves—Jarvis threatened to pull his nose if he did not go about his business—he said "I don't want to quarrel with you, stop till Mr. Jones comes," and he went to attend to a horse's foot that he was examining—I cannot tell whether he pulled the prisoner's nose—I was standing with my back to the fire—Jarvis made a rush at the prisoner to take the hammer from him, and he struck him twice on the head with it.
ALFRED HENRY GRANT ( police-constable G 62.) I took the prisoner—I told him it was for wounding Jarvis—he asked me how he was—I said he was very dangerously ill—he said it was a very bad job; if Jarvis had not interfered with him he should not have done it—I produce the hammer.
ALFRED BOWER I am a surgeon, and live in the Curtain-road. Jarvis was brought to my house on the evening of the 31st of May—he had two wounds on the scalp, and had lost a great deal of blood from them—they were narrow bruised wounds, about two inches long—they did not appear to be very deep—they were such as might be made by this hammer—I dressed them—they were not in themselves dangerous, but were calculated to produce erysipelas.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of a common assault. Aged 32.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1512]
1512. FREDERICKA ASGE was indicted for stealing 10 sovereigns, the monies of James Thomas Roberts, in his dwelling-house.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
CAROLINE ROBERTS . I am the wife of James Thomas Roberts—he is a short hand writer—I know the prisoner—I was introduced to her where she was lodging at my mother's, No. 14, Water-street, Blackfriars—she called herself the Baroness Cushteen—I asked her to pay me a visit—the first time she came to see me was with my mother on the 17th of May—on the 4th of June she came alone—on that afternoon I went into my bedroom, I had to put something into a box—the prisoner went in the bedroom with me—there was a small tin box in the other box—the prisoner asked me what I had got there—I told her it was new silver—there was another small box there, and the prisoner said, "Let me see what there is there"—I told her there was gold in it—she asked me how much—I told her it was not to be counted—I said that because I thought she was too curious—we then went into the other room and had some coffee together—she said "Will you let me look at your keys, as I have lost mine"—I showed them to her, she said they would not suit—she then went home—during the period she had been staying in my room, I had to go out for something, and I left her alone for about ten minutes—I left her in the parlour which communicates with the bedroom by folding doors—I took my keys with me when I went out—on the following morning, Saturday the 5th of June, I had occasion to go to the box about eleven o'clock—I saw there were twenty-six sovereigns in gold—I took one sovereign out—the prisoner came that day about quarter after one o'clock—Mr. Roberts was at home when she came—he went out at half-past
three, and left the prisoner with me—after Mr. Roberts was gone, the prisoner said to me "Will you fetch me some port wine"—I told her I never fetched port wine, but the servant could get it—I sent the servant for it—the prisoner gave me the money, and I gave it to the servant—after the wine was come, the prisoner asked me to get her some ham to eat with the wine—I did not consent at first, but she said I could get it so much better than the servant—I at last went and fetched some—I was gone about twenty minutes—I cannot tell whether I took my keys that time or not, I do not remember—when I came back with the ham the prisoner did not eat any of it—she drank two glasses of wine—the last glass she drank standing, saying she wanted to go home, and she would take the ham with her—she then left—we got into a cab together—directly she got out she wanted to take a drive round the park—I went home with her, and then returned to my own home between seven and eight o'clock—Mr. Roberts came home a little after nine—he had some money which he wanted to put away, and he went to the box—he made a communication to me, I went to the box, and found the lock was strained—Mr. Roberts turned it with a gimblet—he brought the box in which the gold was kept, into the parlour—there were then only fifteen sovereigns in it—I am quite sure there were twenty-five sovereigns in the morning—the silver in the other box had not been touched—I went with Mr. Roberts to the prisoner's lodging—we got there between ten and eleven o'clock—we found the prisoner in bed and all her clothes packed up—she had not said anything to me about leaving her lodging.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. On the Saturday morning, the money was all safe? A. Yes—I thought the prisoner a person of great respectability, the little time I had known her—I do not know whether she came recommended to my mother—she had been staying at the Royal-hotel, in Chatham-place, but I do not know how long—I did not know that she had relations at Bristol—I was away from home for two hours, or two hours and a half, while we went round the parks—we are occupying apartments—I did not secure my room door before I came away on that occasion—I never do so—I partook of the wine with the prisoner—she did not come by my invitation on the Friday—she did on the Saturday—I do not know whether she is a person of a good Polish family or not—we had not agreed that we would take a glass of wine together before she sent for it—she told me she was in trouble about having lost her keys—when I went to her room it was late at night—she was in bed—the officer was with me—I do not know whether she was very much alarmed—there was no one left in my apartments that I know of, during the two hours and a half that I was riding round the park—it was not until my husband came home that we discovered the box had been disturbed.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. It was by her suggestion and her request, that you took the drive for two hours and a half? Yes—there are other lodgers in the house—we have been there five or six weeks—the landlady is here—I left my door open—the outer door was closed—I left the landlady at home—no one could have come in without knocking or ringing—the prisoner said she had lost her keys—I saw some keys found at her lodging.
JAMES THOMAS ROBERTS . I am a short-hand writer—I live at No. 15, Holywell-street, Westminster—I have been in the habit of collecting new money—I keep it in little boxes in a box in the bedroom—I knew of there being 26 l. in gold there—I have heard that my wife took a sovereign out—on Saturday night, 5th June, I had some money to put in the box—I went to
the box, and found the lock had been tampered with—I picked the lock, and found ten sovereigns missing—from what my wife said, I went to the prisoner's lodging, in Water-street, Blackfriars, and took a policeman with me—my wife went into the prisoner's bedroom, and I went in afterwards—the prisoner had then got up—I asked her for those ten sovereigns—she seemed very much surprised, and offered me some of her jewellery, a gold chain, a brooch, some rings, and some money—she had two new sovereigns in a purse, and some silver—I said to her, "Have you got any more money in this room?"—she said, "No"—I asked her several times, and she said, "No"—she offered me her jewellery—she said, "Take this, take this; you may take whatever I have"—she asked Mr. Stonard, my mother-in-law, to lend her 8 l., and she would give that 8 l. to me, with the 2 l. in the purse, which would make up 10 l., and she would repay Mr. Stonard—the officer set to to search the room—I went down stairs, and when I came up the officer produced six sovereigns, wrapped in a piece of paper—these are them—they are all new—the prisoner did not say anything after that—this is the chain she offered me.
Cross-examined. Q. When the prisoner produced her purse and chain, did she not say, "Take this; take all I have got; only leave me till the morning?" A. Yes, she did—some keys of the prisoner's were found in her bed-room—the officer tried them to my box in which the little boxes were—none of them would fit it—when I said to the prisoner, "Give me those ten sovereigns," she said she had no ten sovereigns—I said, "Those ten sovereigns that were taken from my box."
ELEANOR RUSSELL . I am single—I keep the house No. 15, Holywell-street, Westminster. Mr. and Mr. Roberts lodge in my two parlours—I use the front kitchen, which is under the parlour occupied by Mr. Roberts—I remember the prisoner coming there on Friday, the 4th of June—I recollect Mr. Roberts going out—directly after she was gone, I distinctly heard the prisoner walk across the room—I heard the footsteps till they got to the folding-doors—I could not hear them after they passed the folding doors—I then heard them again coming back from the folding-doors—I had missed hearing them for some minutes—I remember the prisoner coming again on Saturday, the 5th of June, and Mr. Roberts going out—immediately she had gone I heard the footsteps again—I heard them nearly all the time she was out—I could not hear them in the bed-room—I lost the trace of the footsteps for some minutes—I remember Mr. Roberts and the prisoner leaving the house together—they left me in the house—no one could have come in without my knocking it—I have a gentleman and lady lodging in the first floor—the gentleman was out—his wife and children were at home—I have a young man and his wife up stairs—the young man was away—my servant was in the house—there is a door from the passage to Mr. Roberts' front parlour, and from that to the bed-room—there is a door from the passage to the bed-room, but that door is kept locked—during the whole time after Mr. Roberts went out with the prisoner till Mr. Roberts' return, I was at home—no one passed across the front parlour while they were away—I am quite sure about that—Mr. Roberts left the servant up stairs when she went out, but she came down as soon as Mr. Roberts was gone—I am certain she did not go into the room—I do not believe anybody could have gone across the floor—I heard no one.
WILLIAM MILLERMAN ( police-constable B 95.) I went to the prisoner's lodging with Mr. and Mr. Roberts—the prisoner was in bed—Mr. Roberts went into the room first, then Mr. Roberts—I waited some time, and he
called me in—I saw a purse on the table—it contained two new sovereigns and some silver—I told the prisoner I must take her into custody on suspicion of stealing ten sovereigns—she said she had no more money—she said that several times—I am quite sure about that—I searched the purse—the two sovereign, 5 s. 10 d. in silver, and 5 d. in coppers, were altogether in one end of the purse—the other end was empty—I searched, and found six new sovereigns in this paper, under the glass on the table in the bed-room—it was a long time before I found them—they must have been put underneath the glass by some means—the prisoner did not say anything when they were produced—I found these three small keys inside her portmanteau—the lock of the portmanteau was off—I found the prisoner was ready to move—everything was packed up—there was nothing about the house.
MRS. STONARD. I am Mr. Roberts' mother. The prisoner was a lodged of mine—she was recommended to me by some waiter at the hotel—he said she was very respectable—she gave her name Madam Asge—she talked several times of leaving—I had no notion she was going to leave in the morning—she told me on the Saturday that she was going to Bristol on Tuesday or Wednesday—I was very angry with her on the Saturday for being out all day—I was afraid she was ill, as she was not in good health—when I went into her room with Mr. and Mr. Roberts I found she had packed up everything, but previous to that, the things had all been in confusion.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know she had an uncle living at Bristol? A. She told me she had; then she told me afterwards he was not her uncle—she spoke English very badly; I could understand her—she spoke it better after she had been with me than she did before, but she spoke it imperfectly.
Witnesses for the Defence.
FREDERICK. BEHR . I am head waiter at the Royal Hotel, Chatham-place; it is kept by Mr. Keyser. I remember the prisoner coming there in the month of March—while she was there she went once to Bristol—I suppose she was there, because I had a letter from her with the Bristol post-mark on it—after she had staid some time at our house I recollect her going to a lodging in water-street—she came to the hotel almost every day to dine at the table d' hote —during the time she was at the hotel she paid her bills regularly—I think the last time she paid was in the month of May—I think it was more than a week before this transaction—Mr. Keyser very often lends money to his customers, if they require it—I recollect the prisoner preparing to leave London—I understood she was going to Bristol—she did not name any day—when she first came she did not speak a word of English; out servants were obliged to call me to interpret—I never spoke English to her—she spoke German—in consequence of something that passed between us, I lent her some money—I promised it to her on the Wednesday, but I sent it her on Friday morning, the 4th of June—it was six sovereigns—they were quite new—I cannot say whether these six sovereigns, in this paper, are my sovereigns, but I should say they were—I sent them in a note which I wrote, in which the six sovereigns are mentioned, and sent it to her by the hand of Kester—this is the note—( looking at it)—I have seen her wear jewellery—I think I saw this chain on her—I had not seen it during the latter week or fortnight of her being there, but in the early part I know very well I have seen a chain similar to this, and I have seen this ring.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You seem to be intimate with your master's customers?
A. Yes—I am head-waiter—there was nothing peculiar in my intimacy with this lady—any lady who comes and wants 6 l. at any time, if I like, I can give it her—I have very often done such a thing before, without any particular introduction, if the persons are well recommended—this lady was not recommended to me personally, but she was recommended to the house by Mr. Bloom from Hamburg—he was a lodger in our house—I do not know what he is—I had never seen the prisoner before she came to the hotel on the 23rd of March—Mr. Bloom was at our house about a year before the prisoner came—she did not bring any letters—I do not know whether she is married—when I lend money I do not inquire whether a woman is married—she looked very respectable—it was on account of her looks I lent it her—I had lent her 1 l. once before when she went the first time to Bristol—she could not speak a word of English when she first came—I think she could not understand it—the servants have been obliged to call met to speak to her—I offered her this money on the Wednesday—when she paid her first bill she said, "If I should want any money perhaps I can have it"—her first bill was perhaps about 4 l.—I sent this money by our porter's wife, and a note—I do not recollect the manner in which I wrapped up the sovereigns—I do not know whether they were wrapped up small or large—I do not know whether it was in the shape of a note—I do not recollect whether the sovereigns shook about—there was no one present when I wrapped them up—I took these sovereigns out of my own money—I did not tell my master—I think I told the prosecutor I had known the prisoner in Poland—that was not true—I did not know her there—I said so because our commissioner took the lodging for her, and she told me I should not give her direction to any one, because she was so miserable and so badly off—she wanted me not to tell where she lived—I had some conversation with the prosecutor.
MR. PARNELL. Q. That was when Mr. Roberts came, and the policeman in plain clothes? A. Yes—the prisoner had paid her money well, and conducted herself decently before—my master can prove that—the money I lent her before, she had repaid—I recollect that the six sovereigns I sent her were new, because I had put them aside—I think I recollect the person from whom I received them—he asked how much his bill was, because he wanted to get some money from the Bank—I recollect the money was now—I paid some of it to my young master, and he said, "How remarkably bright these are!"—I gave the six sovereigns to Mary Kester—I have no acknowledgment of the receipt of them from the prisoner.
MARY KESTER ( through an interpreter.) I am the wife of Philip Kester. I am employed at the Royal Hotel, Chatham-place—Mr. Behr sent me with a parcel to the prisoner, on the Thursday or Friday before this matter happened—I gave it to the prisoner—I saw her open the parcel, and she said, "How excellent good this man is to lend me 6 l."—she opened the paper and showed it me—I saw six new sovereigns just the same as they would come from the Mint—I recollect a fire taking place in Water-street, four or five days before—it was not far from the prisoner's lodging—I went to the prisoner's lodging after the fire was over—my husband told me to go to see if I could assist—when I went there, the prisoner had lost her keys, and did not know what had become of them—the keys were wanted to open a trunk—my husband went to her with his keys—I do not know whether they answered the purpose—I remember taking a gold chain to the prisoner on Saturday evening, because she was going to leave on Monday for Bristol.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. From whom did you receive the gold chain to give
her? A. From my husband, between nine and ten o'clock in the evening—the little parcel I took to the prisoner, with the money, was folded up and sealed—it was about as large as my two fingers—when I saw it opened there were two papers, one was enveloped and the other was written on—the sovereign were in the paper that was written on.
MR. PARNELL. Q. Did you see the note? A. Yes; this is it ( looking at the one produced.)
PHILIP KESTER . I am housekeeper at the Royal Hotel. There are two houses, Nos. 6 and 2—I am housekeeper at No. 2—the prisoner staid there some time, and then went into lodgings—I recollect a fire taking place near her lodging—I went to her shortly afterwards—I look some keys with me, and tried to open a trunk—she had lost her own keys—I cannot recollect when the fire took place—it was some days before the Saturday on which this matter happened—I recollect sending my wife to the prisoner on the Saturday evening with a chain—she had told me four days before, that she would go to Bristol—she did not know what day she should go—she told me on the Wednesday to send the chain, and she said something about it on the Saturday morning between eleven and twelve o'clock.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Where did you take the chain from? A. Oct of pawn between seven and nine o'clock in the evening—I paid the money from my own pocket, 2 l. 16 s. 3 d.—I did not owe the prisoner any money—I lent her the money because I read her letters she received from Germany, and I took her for a respectable lady—I thought I should get my money, with interest—I did not take a receipt from her—the pawnbroker's is in Fleet-street—I do not know the No.—I took the chain to the hotel, and gave it to my wife to take to the prisoner—I was afraid to take it to her myself—I must not go out of the house without permission, because my wife cannot speak English.
CONSTANCE KEYSER . I am proprietor of the Royal Hotel. There are two houses, No. 2, and No. 6; one of them is kept by Philip Kester—I recollect the prisoner coming there in March—she came recommended—I do not admit customers generally without they are recommended—while she was there, she was in the habit of dining at the table d' hote almost every day, and she continued to do so four or five times a week after she left—she conducted herself very respectably indeed—these articles of jewellery are hers—she was always sitting beside me, and I recollect them all very well—I recollect Mr. Bloom of Bristol—he is her uncle—she was preparing to leave my house on the 5th of June to go Bristol.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You would have lent her this money? A. Yes; of course, and a deal more—it was not very strange that she should apply to a waiter—my waiters are in the habit of lending money sometimes, and I am myself—the prisoner's name is Fredericka Asge in my book—I always called her "Madam"—I considered her to be married.
MR. PARNELL. Q. You have kept the hotel some time? A. About four years—it is not unusual for persons who keep foreign hotels to lend money to their customers, if they want it—they come very often with foreign money to the house—the prisoner always paid
JOSEPH BLOOM . I am a wholesale agent, and live at Bristol. The prisoner is my niece—she is married—her name is Asge—she was in Bristol a month or two ago—I was expecting her at the time of the time of this transaction—she wrote me a letter that she would be in Bristol on Friday or Saturday, but she was waiting for letters from home—she wrote this note ( looking at one) to me, and I was in
consequence expecting her in Bristol—she wished me to take lodgings for her—she left a few pounds with me when she left me in March last—she has been an honest respected person.
MR. BALLANTINE, Q. Where do you live? A. No. 8, Bath-parade—I have lived there several years and carry on business—I have rooms at the back of the premises, and keep my goods there—I have fancy goods, both English and foreign—the prisoner's mother married my brother—her father is dead—I believe her mother is living—I received the prisoner at Bristol—I did not inquire why she had left her husband—she has not left her husband—she came here on pleasure—I discovered this note, which was sent to her with the money—Mr. Keyser wished to go to her lodging—the servants said the money that was sent to her was in a note—I went there with Mr. Keyser—I had nothing in my hand—I saw some paper there—I did not hear directions given by the attorney that no body should touch them but the police-constable—I did not reply "that was the proper way"—I took up some papers in presence of the policeman—I believe he was kneeling down at the trunk—his back was not turned—I did not touch the papers that were on the box—I touched some papers—I did not say to the constable, "I am sure the letter is amongst these papers"—I was not remonstrated with for taking up some papers—the policeman produced some papers from a trunk—he took them to a table—I did not touch those papers while he was kneeling down—I did not hear the attorney's clerk say to me, "Where did you get those papers from?"—he did not say to me, "I distinctly told you that no paper should be touched except by the policeman, who will show them, and afford every facility for their inspection"—I did not say, "I took them out of this basket"—there was no basket there—I said, "There is a carpet-bag, there may be something in there"—I opened them in the presence of Mr. Roberts—the man took a lot of paper from the trunk and brought them to the window-table, in presence of Mr. Keyser, and Mr. Roberts—Mr. Keyser said, "That is the note—that is the note."
MR. PARNELL. Q. The note shown to you was taken charge of by the policeman? A. Yes—I did not take another note with me, and substitute it for this note—I swear solemnly I did not place that note there.
GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 28.— Confined Twelve Months.
ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18470614-1513]
1513. HENRY SIDA and JOSEPH MILES were indicted for stealing 1 sack, value 6 d.; and 39 ploughshares; the goods of Joseph Choat, and another.
MR. COLLIER conducted the Prosecution.
EDWARD GODDARD . I am in the employ of Philip Choat, and another, who have a farm in the parish of Barking. Last Thursday evening, I left work between six and seven o'clock, I had used this sack that day to put over my back as it rained—I left it under the manger—there were two or three dozen ploughshares outside the stable when I left work—I returned next morning between four and five, and missed all but two of them, and the sack.
JOBLLUA HITCHMAN ( police-constable K 362.) Last Friday morning,
soon after four o'clock, I saw the prisoners coming through the gateway of the third field from the prosecutor's barn—Sida had a sack on his back—they both ran away on seeing me—I ran and secured Miles, and brought him back to the sack which Sida had thrown down—it was marked "Choat," and contained thirty-nine ploughshares—he said he had found them on the footpath
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Were they in the high road? A. No, crossing the fields—not in the footway, but in the wagon way, leading straight from the barn—I never saw people on that road before—they were coming in a direction form the barn, and not a quarter of a mile from it.
GEORGE CORBIN ( police constable K 100.) I apprehended Sida, on the 11th, Friday morning, at a quarter to six o'clock, in a field about a mile and a half from the farm—I said, I took him on suspicion of stealing plough-shares from Messrs. Choat—he said, he knew nothing of it.
EDWARD GODDARD re-examined. This is my master's sack—I know it by two holes which I cut in it to put a string through, and Messrs. Choat's name is upon it—the ploughshares are the same kind as ours.
PHILIP CHOAT . I am in partnership with my brother, this is our sack—the ploughshares have the mark of our maker, and fit our ploughs.
SIDA GUILTY . Aged 20.
MILES GUILTY . Aged 36.
Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1517. HENRY MAYS was indicted for unlawfully trafficking in, for gain, a certain Register ticket, issued to one James Thompson, pursuant to the provisions of the 8th Victoria; to which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18470614-1518]
1518. ELIZA DAVIS was indicted for feloniously assaulting Henry Davilen, and putting him in fear, and stealing from his person, and against his will, 1 purse, value 3 d.; 2 half-crowns, and 10 shillings, his property; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
HENRY DAVILEN . I am in the Artillery, at Woolwich. On Sunday, the 9th of May, about two o'clock in the afternoon, I met the prisoner in Woolwich—I went into a house with her—she sat me down on a seat, put her head through the window, and called in two girls—just as they came in she struck me between the shoulders with a poker, and knocked me down—the other girls got a broomstick, and the prisoner laid on me with a poker, and a broomstick—she put her hand into my pocket, and took out my purse—this is it—there were two half-crowns and ten shillings in small silver in it—I was sober—I had only drunk a pint of beer.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you knock me on the floor, and say you would have what you wanted, that you had only four pennyworth of copper, and you would give me half-a-crown next day? A. No—none of that is true—I did not take a purse out of your bosom—the girls did not come to your assistance, but to assist you to abuse me.
JOHN WALKER ( police-constable K 298.) On this Sunday evening I heard a call, went to the house, and saw Davilen there—I saw the prisoner in sight of the door—I did not go into the house—she was given in charge—she gave me the purse, and said it belonged to one of the gunners of the Artillery, and not to the prosecutor.
Prisoner. Q. How much was in it? A. Half-a-crown and 1 s. when I took it.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent; the prosecutor has perjured himself, he took the purse out of my bosom.
WILLIAM CLARKE HUNT . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted, Sept. 1845, and confined six months)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18470614-1519]
1519. WILLIAM DYBLE was indicted for unlawfully assaulting Henry Wadlow, a peace-officer, in the execution of his duty.
MR. CLERKE conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM THORN . I have charge of the Crown and Anchor public-house, at Woolwich. I act as landlord—On the 9th of April, at eleven o'clock in the evening, the defendant came there with a man named Glover—they wanted drink—I did not serve them, as they were in liquor—they had been there twice before that day—they were not making a noise, but I did not want, them in the house—they wanted to go into the tap-room—I would not let them—I called a policeman, who came to the front door—he did not come in, but in consequence of what he said I tried to get the men to leave my house—I went to Glover, and desired him to walk out—he took me by the collar before I had done anything to him, an knocked me up against the wall, and tried to throw me on the floor—Wadlow, the policeman, was at the door, in such a position as he could see it—he came in to assist me, and took Glover from me—Dyble then took me by the collar and threw me over the table—the policeman got Glover out of the house—Dyble followed of himself.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Are you landlord of the public-house? A. No, superintendent—I do the business—I am barman—I asked them civilly to go out at first, and then I tried to turn them out—the police-man told me to turn them out—I always put my shutters up at eleven o'clock—they were up, and the door half closed—there were no other people
there drinking—there was no one in the tap-room or bar—there were some gentlemen in the coffee-room—the defendant formerly worked at baking—he has been a fly-man—there were no other fly-men drinking there—this is the only house for fly-men to stop at—I do not know whether they would have paid for the drink if I had let them have it—I did not hear Dyble say, "Come away, don't be a fool."
MR. CLERKE. Q. Have you charge of the house? A. I am barman—there is a landlady living there, but no landlord.
COURT. Q. Did you do anything to the prisoner and Glover? A. Nothing.
HENRY WADLOW ( policeman.) On the 9th of April I was on duty at Woolwich. Thorn came to me—in consequence of what he said, I went to the door of the Crown and Anchor—I saw Thorn take hold of Glover by the arms and say, "You must go out"—Glover immediately caught him by the collar, and made use of the most beastly expressions—he took Thorn by the collar, threw him back with great violence, and succeeded in getting into the tap-room—Dyble was there—he did nothing then—they were both together—I took Glover into custody, and took him outside into the street—he then struck me in the eye, and knocked me backwards—I fell backwards, he fell on me—I still held him by the collar, while he was on me—Dyble came out of the house, and said, "You b—r, let him go," he caught me by my thumb, pressed it backwards from Glover's collar, and kicked me in the side—I became insensible—I had previous to that called out for assistance, and while I was insensible two civilians came to my assistance, and pulled Dyble off me—both the men were on me then—my hand is very much hurt—the skin is torn off—I was off duty two days—both the men had been drinking—I kept hold of Glover—Dyble made his escape, and was taken next morning.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe it was an accident that your hand was hurt? A. Yes, it was trampled on by one of them—the house was not closed—the shutters were up—it was eleven o'clock at night, and was rather dark—Glover knocked me down, fell on me, knelt on me, and pressed his knuckles against my throat—I struck him slightly with my staff—I had got up on my knees when Dyble came up—I am sure Dyble is the person—I had seen him inside—I have no recollection of fainting more than once; that was when I received the injury on my thumb, and the kick—I was on the ground then—some gentleman came to the station and complained of the conduct of these men—I know a man named Matterson, he was not there in my presence—I did not charge him with being the man who kicked me—he said on the following morning, "You might as well say it was me"—I can-not swear Dyble did not follow me to the station door, but he was not inside that night—there were two or three people standing drinking in the public-house, in front of the bar—I did not see them touch Thorn—I am sure it was the prisoner, and not Glover, who laid hold of my thumb—it was not so dark but that I could see that.
MR. CLERKE. Q. How long have you known Dyble? A. A few months—I was knocked down by Glover, and got up on my knees, and was thrown on my back a second time by the violence I received from Dyble—I had not fainted before he came up.
JAMES SOLOMON . I live in Woolwich, near the Crown and Anchor. On the 9th of April I saw Wadlow in his knees—Dyble kicked him on the side or the arms, I cannot tell which, because the light over the door was out—I
knew Dyble, by seeing him behind the busses—I as sure he is the man—assistance came, and the men were taken off.
Cross-examined. Q. You live next door but one to Mr. Thorn? A. Yes, at an eating-house—it was dark—Dyble was outside when I came up, somewhere between the kerb-stone and the door, not many yards off Glover and the policeman—he did not go to them till after they had struggled some time—I did not hear him say, "Come away, don't be a fool!"—they had been on the ground a few minutes before he came up—I was there when Wadlow appeared to lose his senses—I did not assist him to the station, or go there.
THOMAS EDWARD DRINKWATER . I am a surgeon, and live at Woolwich. On the 9th of April Wadlow called at my surgery—he was considerably bruised about the forehead, and had two black eyes—his right hand was a good deal torn and lacerated—the skin had been rubbed of the knuckles—the thumb of his left hand was severely sprained, which was followed by a good deal of inflammation—he complained of pain in his chest—the injuries were such as to keep him from duty several days.
Cross-examined. Q. How long was he off duty? A. About five days—the main cause of that was the sprained thumb—there was no serious internal injury, or any appearance on the arm or side which made it necessary for him to keep away from duty.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Four Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1521. THOMAS FRANCIS was indicted for stealing 1 pair of trowsers, value 10 s., 1 neckerchief, 6 d.; 2 cheroots, 2 d.; and 1 shilling; the property of John Robins; to which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18470614-1522]
1522. JAMES LEE JONES was indicted for stealing 2 half-crowns, 3 shillings, and one 1 1/2 d. piece, the monies of Job Charlton, from his person.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
JOB CHARLTON . I am a beer-seller, and live in Hog-lane, Greenwich, On Monday, the 7th of June, the prisoner came to my house between five and six o'clock—he and I afterwards had some conversation together—I am clear that I had at that time 8 s. and a 1 1/2 d. silver coin in my pocket—I did not give away or put away from my pocket that money—no one but the prisoner sufficiently near me to have taken it from me—the prisoner went away about seven o'clock—in about a quarter of an hour after, I found my money was gone—I went after the prisoner—I found him; and charged him with having my money—he said to the police-officer, "I have some money, but what money I have, that man gave me for adulterating his beer"—the money was produced, and it was mine.
Prisoner. Q. Did I sleep at your house on Monday night? A. Yes, you did—I did not ask you if you were acquainted with any person at Green-wich—I did not walk with you to find a man at Greenwich College.
Prisoner. I got up the next morning at half-past six o'clock, and adulterated a barrel of porter for you, and you gave me 12 s. Witness. I did not.
Prisoner. You did; I called again on the Tuesday, and sat and drank in your parlour; you offered me a purse, and said, "I will give you this purse;" I said, "I will not take the purse, let me see what it contains;" you took out 8 s. and gave me, and said I owed you for half-a-gallon of 6 d. ale; I offered you a shilling; you refused it; I offered it to your wife; she took it.
COURT. Q. Have you any servants? A. Yes—they must have seen it if the prisoner had adulterated my beer—I did not give him any money.
JOSEPH JOHN COOK ( police-constable R 354.) I took the prisoner at the prosecutor's suggestion—I found on him this silver and this 1 1/2 d. piece.
JOB CHARLTON . re-examined. This is the 1 1/2 d. piece I had—I lost 8 s., as near as I can recollect—here is 7 s. 1 farthing, and this 1 1/2 d. piece.
Prisoner's Defence. I told him if he did not satisfy me, I knew the way to get it; he accompanied me down to the cellar that morning; I became a servant of his to do it; he had a red pan with water in it in his cellar; he sent his servant out of the way.
JOSEPH JOHN COOK re-examined. I believe they sent the Excise down, but they could not find that any beer had been adulterated.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1523]
1523. ISHMAEL ISRAEL was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 3 l.; and 1 watch-chain, 6 d.; the goods of William Henry Cheeseman.
WILLIAM HENRY CHEESEMAN . I live in Greenwich-market. On Sunday, the 16th of May, I went to a water-closet—I hung my watch on a nail, went away, and forgot it—I went there in half-an-hour—my watch was gone—I received information, and went to look after the prisoner—he said he did not steal the watch—he said, taking a penny spoon would be stealing, but taking the watch from the water-closet was not stealing—my watch was found on him.
Cross-examined by Mr. O'BRIEN. Q. Where was the water-closet? A. Adjoining the house—it was not locked—it had been used by the public.
WILLIAM HARVEY ( police-sergeant R 4.) This watch was found on the prisoner, and brought to the station by a constable who was dismissed the next day—the prisoner said he took the watch from the water-closet, and that was not stealing.
GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1524]
1524. JOHN DINGWALL was indicted for stealing 1 loaf of bread, value 1 d.; the goods of Richard Farr.
RICHARD FARR . I am a baker, and live at Woolwich. On the 7th of June the prisoner took a 1 d. loaf out of my window—I collared him and brought him back—he did not make any remark.
SAMUEL JEFFRY . I know the prisoner, and have witnessed his conduct—he is very inoffensive, but of weak mind, an does not know what he is about.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1525]
1525. JOHN SPENCE, WILLIAM SWAIN and THOMAS JOHNSON were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of John Roche Williams, from his person; and that Swain and Johnson had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN ROCHE WILLIAMS . I am a boot-closer, and live in Hampstead-road. On the evening of the 24th of May I was at Greenwich-fair—the officer spoke to me, and I found my handkerchief was gone—I had seen it safe about an hour before—this now produced is it.
Swain. Q. What do you swear to it by? A. By the holes in it—there are about four holes—I won't swear to it exactly.
COURT. Q. Was your handkerchief like this in every respect? A. Yes, exactly the same pattern.
MICHAEL HAYDON ( City police-constable, No. 274.) On the 24th of May, about seven o'clock in the evening, I saw the three prisoners walking together for about a quarter of an hour, at Greenwich fair—I then saw Spence take a handkerchief from Mr. Williams's pocket and pass it to Swain—Johnson was behind, covering them—as soon as he did it they ran away together behind some booths—I told the prosecutor, and then went after the prisoners—they were all three together—I took them—I had seen them watching people's pockets as they were walking up and down the fair.
Swain. Q. Did you see me with these two prisoners? A. Yes—I found the handkerchief on you—I suppose I was a couple of yards from you when the handkerchief was taken—you went behind the booths the moment it was taken.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) I found this handkerchief on Swain—two other handkerchiefs were found on Johnson.
Johnson. They were what I fetch my food in; I was only discharged from prison the Saturday before.
Spence. I saw Swain buy this handkerchief.
Swain's Defence. I was discharged from the county prison on the Saturday, and on Whit-Monday I went to the fair with a young man; I happened to miss him, and I met with this lad Johnson; he asked me whether I would stand some beer; I took him into a public-house and stood a pot of beer; a young man came in with a handkerchief and asked me whether I would buy it; he wanted 1 s. 6 d. for it, and I gave it him; I was coming out of the public-house, the constable saw me, and knowing me by giving me six months at another prison, he took me, and asked me whether I had the handkerchief; another officer said he watched me, and I was by myself; I was with a sailor.
MICHAEL HAYDON re-examined I had been Swain by himself about three hours before.
Johnson's Defence I was discharged from prison on the Saturday before; I was trying to get an honest living when the officer came and took me; Swaine said he was going to have a pot of beer, and he would give me some.
MICHAEL HAYDON re-examined I produce a certificate of Swain's former conviction at this court—( read—Convicted 23 rd Nov, 1846, confined six months)—the prisoner Swain is the person.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( City police-constable, No. 122.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner Johnson's former conviction—( read—Convicted 23 rd Nov. 1846, confired six months)—the prisoner is the person.
SPENCE — GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Three Months.
SWAIN — GUILTY . Aged 23.
JOHNSON — GUILTY . Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
