1527. CHARLES PEMBURY was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 2 l. 10 s., and 1 watch-chain, 1 d.; the goods of Thomas Davis and that he had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS DAVIS I live in Sun-alley, Woolwich, and am a sawyer. On 26th of May, about eight o'clock in the morning, I went into the White Lion public-house—the prisoner was there in the parlour—I took my watch out of my pocket to wind it up—the prisoner said he would take it to the bar, and have it put to rights for me—I said, "No, let it alone; I shall put it to rights myself"—he took it out of my hand, and went off with it.
Prisoner. He gave it into my hand, and told me to go and pawn it. Witness No, I did not—he did not bring anything back for it—I was not the worse for liquor—I have known him ever since he was born—I thought he might bring it back.
GEORGE BARTON . I am in the employ of Mr. Carter, a pawnbroker, at Greenwich. On the 26th of May the prisoner came, and brought this watch—he pawned it for 12 s., in the name of Thomas Smith—he said it was his own.
JOHN GRIFFIN ( police-constable R 229.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 3rd of June—I told him he was charged with stealing a watch from the prosecutor—he replied that he had settled matters with the prosecutor—I said I should take him to the station, and I asked what he had done with the watch—he said he had pawned it at Greenwich, and had lost the duplicate—he did not tell me at what shop he pawned it.
JOHN WALKER ( police-constable R 298.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at Maidstone—( read—Convicted 9 th March, 1846, and confined six months)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY Aged 28.— Confined One Year.
SURREY CASES.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18470614-1528]
1528. JAMES COOK was indicted for stealing 4 bushels of oats, value 15 s.; and 1 sack, 2 s., the goods of Henry Dunkin, in a barge on the navigable river Thames.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
DANIEL DAVIS . I live in Two Kings-row, Horsleydown, and am a watch-man in the employ of Messrs. Courage and Donaldson. On the evening of the 20th of April, about nine o'clock, I was in the neighbourhood of Horsley-down-stairs—the prosecutor, Mr. Dunkin, had two or three barges lying of there—I met the prisoner and James Bennett, who has been convicted—Bennett had a sack of oats, and the prisoner had an empty sack under his arm, and a hat, which I believe was Bennett's—(Bennett had no hat on)—the prisoner was in front—I stopped Bennett—the prisoner turned back, and asked what I had to do with Bennett, and held up his fist to me—I called out, "Police"—the prisoner dropped his empty bag, and ran away—the sack that Bennett had, contained oats.
DAVID DAVIS . I am in the employ of Courage and Donaldson. I heard the cry of "Police" that night—I saw the prisoner and Bennett—the prisoner walked first, with a hat in his hand and a sack under his arm—Davis caught hold of Bennett, and the prisoner went off when they hallooed out for a policeman, and left his bag behind him.
Prisoner. Q. Which arm did I have the sack under? A. I do not know—you had it under your arm, and dropped it—I let you pass me near the Rose and Crown.
GEORGE TERRY . I am in the employ of Mr. Henry Dunkin, a corndealer and lightman. He had some barges at Davis's-wharf, Horsleydown—there were eighty sacks of oats in one barge—at nine o'clock that night I received information, and missed one sack of oats—this is it—it had the same kind of oats in it that was in the barge; I had left the barge about half an hour.
Prisoner's Defence I had nothing to do with it; I was at Horsleydown, and came up the stairs; I heard Davis speak; I turned to see what it was, and he had hold of Bennett; I walked back; a man came and said, "Cook, I must have you;" I said, "I have done nothing;" I went away.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1529]
1529. JAMES HARLEY was indicted for stealing 1 bag, value 1 d.; 1 half-sovereign, 1 sixpence, and 3 pence; the property of William Payne; from his person.
WILLIAM PAYNE . I am lodging at Mr. Harris', in Point-street, Portsmouth—on the 12th of May I was on Wimbledon Common, on my way to Portsmouth—I was near some trees—the prisoner came up to me with another man, who asked me if I was on the road—I said "No," I was standing there because it was raining—the prisoner then asked me if I wanted to buy a razor—I said no, I did not know that I did, what sort was it—he took it out of his pocket and showed it to me—I was going to buy it—I took a small bag out of my pocket which contained a halfsovereign, a sixpence, and three pence in copper—I was going to get the threepence out of the bag, and the prisoner caught the bag out of my hand—I was going to catch him, and the other man said if I touched him he would give me some cold steel—they went off for thirty or forty yards, then ran as hard as they could—I stood under the trees till they were out of sight, and then pulled off my smock-frock and followed them—I got sight of them a long way a-head, and followed them for five or six miles—when the prisoner got to Kingston he went into a baker's shop—I followed behind a cart and passed the shop—I then saw him come out of the shop and come up towards London again—I gave information to an officer, who took him—I can swear he is the man that took my bag.
THOMAS HOLLOWAY ( police-constable V 226.) On the 12th of May Payne gave me information—I proceeded in pursuit of the prisoner, and saw him at the corner of Coomb's-lane—I asked him whether he had not offered a razor for sale, and while I was speaking to him the prosecutor came up and said, "That is the man that robbed me"—the prisoner said, "I have not got the money, the other man has got it"—I took the prisoner to the station—I found nothing on him—this is the razor Payne gave me, which he said he had of the prisoner.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18470614-1530]
1530. ARTHUR LEARY and THOMAS WYATT were indicted for unlawfully receiving from Edward Ransby the sum of 2 s. 6 d., under pretence of helping him to recover part of the sum of 197 l. 10 s., the amount of an order stolen from him by John Jenner, ( see page 417,) they not having caused Jenner to be brought to trial for the said felony.
MR. PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
EDWARD RANSBY . I live at Bawsey, in Suffolk. On Monday, the 26th of April, I was in town—I went to the Yorkshire Stingo, Blackfriars-road, that afternoon—I had a check for 197 l. 10 s., and 18 s. or 19 s.—I parted with that check to John Jenner, the landlord of the house, and made an arrangement for him to meet me at the bank the following morning, to restore it to me—he did not meet me, and I went to his house—I ultimately gave him in charge on the 27th of April—we went before a Magistrate, and I charged him with robbing me of my check—the Magistrate heard my statement, and had Jenner remanded—having left the police-office, I went with the constable to search different houses after the person who had cashed the check—while we were going, I saw the two prisoners, (I had seen Leary at the Yorkshire Stingo the day before, but he was not in the room at the time I gave the check to Jenner)—Leary asked me how I was getting on; I said I hoped to transport the landlord and one or two others, if I could get them—he said, "I suppose you won't care about that, if you could get your money returned"—I said I should not like to let him off so—he said I should not get my money if I went on with it—he said, "I suppose you would rather have your money"—I said, "Yes; I should like to get my money back"—he said, "If you will go down in the country in the morning, you shall have your money returned, every shilling"—in order to get my money, I agreed to go down in the country in the morning, and I said I should detain one of them while the other fetched the money—Leary stopped with me, and Wyatt went and fetched the money—he was away half an hour, or three-quarters of an hour—when he came back he told me he had got a party waiting outside with the money; if I would write him out a receipt for the money, he would go and fetch it—I wrote out a receipt for the full amount of the check, and gave it him—this is the receipt I wrote for 197 l. 10 s.—he went out and brought me in a bag which contained the money as I thought—I found in it 191 l. all in gold—the prisoners told me the sooner I went away the better, and advised me to go as soon as possible for fear I should lose my money again—I wanted to go as soon as I could, to carry my money home—Leary said if I was not in town the next day, Jenner would be liberated, and I did not know any better—I suppose, that was why I was to go in the country, that he might be liberated—I did not make any remark—I went into the country on the following morning—I did not go before the Magistrate—it was in consequence of my receiving this money that I did not go before the Magistrate—on the 1st of May, Wyatt came down to my house at Bawsey; he said he had come down on behalf of Jenner and his wife and family, and I should be doing them a great favour if I did not attend at the Court to go on with the prosecution—he said that Jenner's attorney had told him that if the letter the summons was in, was not rightly directed, I might seal it up and send it back, and they would not know it, and I was not obliged to open it if it were not rightly directed—I said I intended to go on with the prosecution—I intended to come up the following morning, as I had received the summons—I have not
the summons here—I had a constable, and took Wyatt into custody—I came up with him to London on the following morning.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. At that time you had your money? A. I had 191 l.—I went before the Magistrate with Jenner; I saw the clerk writing—I cannot tell whether he wrote down what I said—I forget whether what I said was read over to me—I first saw Leary on the 26th of April—Wyatt was not with him then—Leary said nothing to me on the 26th—I saw him alone at various times in the course of the 27th of April—I first saw him with Wyatt on that evening—I met them in the Borough—we went to a public-house, where the money was returned—I did not see Leary afterwards till he was given into custody.
COURT. Q. Were both the prisoners present when that expression was used, "If you go in the country in the morning you shall have every shilling?"— A. Yes, both.
MR. PARRY. Q. Were you absent from any examination? A. Yes; on the following day—I live with my father-in-law, a publican, he farms a little land—it was his check—it did not belong to me at all—I am twenty-three years old.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Where was the money returned? A. At a public-house in the Borough—I do not know what house—the money was returned in a bag, which was tied with a string—Wyatt went out to get it, and brought it tied—after I had got the money, he said I had better go away as soon as I could, for fear I should lose it again—I did not see Wyatt again while a I was in town—he came down to my house in the country—he was not in the public-house where I was robbed—I did not see him till I met him at the time he brought back the money—I knew that Jenner's attorney was a person named Roberts—I had seen Roberts attending for Jenner at the Police-court—when Wyatt-came down in the country and mentioned Jenner's attorney, I knew to whom he alluded—Wyatt told me, that Jenner's attorney had told him, that if the letter which contained the summons was not directed right, I need not attend to it, but might re-seal it and send it back.
HENRY BARRY ( police-constable M 171.) I took Leary into custody in Martin-street, Union-street, Blackfriars-road, which is where he lives—I told him he was charged with being concerned with Jenner and Wyatt in compromising a felony—he said he done it to get a few shillings for himself—he did not say with whom he went.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Is Mr. Woodhouse a sergeant of your division? A. Yes—Mr. Cottingham, the Magistrate, directed me to take Leary—I know Mr. Robert's by seeing him attend the Police-court, that is all—I never had any communication with him about this matter—Mr. Cottingham ordered me to get a summons, and write a letter into the country, which I did—that was given out in open Court, Roberts might have heard it—I and Sergeant Woodhouse were at the station when Wyatt was brought there—I did not see Roberts there—I did not see him in company with Wyatt.
MR. PARNELL. Q. When was this given out in Court? A. On the 28th of April—Jenner was before the Magistrate, the prosecutor did not attend, and the Magistrate said, "You had better write a letter, and enclose a summons," which I did.
Mr. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you never been in Mr. Robert's house? A. I never have—I know who you mean, he is a solicitor—I know where he
lives—I never met him at Jenner's—I never was in Jenner's house but once, when I went with Sergeant Woodhouse to search the house.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-sergeant M 13.) I produce this receipt.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You know Mr. Roberts, the attorney? A. Yes—I have not seen him to-day—the case was postponed last Sessions, in consequence of Robert's absence—I do not recollect some person who said he came from Roberts, promising that he should be here next Sessions—I heard Roberts called on his subpœna—I think I saw him this day week, at Southwark Police-court—I know him perfectly well by attending the Court—I do not visit him—I was never at his house—I do not think I saw him in company with Wyatt.
MR. BALLANTINE to EDWARD RANSNY. Q. Did Wyatt say, "You need not be afraid of me, I am a hard-working man, the person who got the check cashed is an honest man, and had no idea of defrauding any one by so doing?" A. Yes, that was at the public-house—I did not say that I believed Wyatt was an innocent man, and promise to give him 1 l. when he came out of prison—I said I did not know whether he was innocent or not, but he knew where he got the money.
MR. BALLANTINE called
JANE SAUNDERS . My husband is a watch-maker, and lives at No. 1, John-place, Blackfriars-road—I have known Wyatt very well for fourteen or fifteen years—he is a very old acquaintance of my husband's—I saw the check on the Monday night—my husband had it—I knew of his changing it the next morning—I was not present when he received it—I understood from him that he got it from Jenner—about an hour after my husband returned with the money in the morning, I heard there was a charge of stealing the check—I knew my husband had got the money—I went with him to the prosecutor to give the money back—I gave the money to Wyatt, just outside the public-house door—it was in a bag, tied up, just as my husband received it at the Bank—I never went into the public-house.
MR. PARNELL. Q. Where is your husband living? A. At No. 1, John-place—he has been away ever since this matter happened—he went away on the Wednesday following, after the money was given—I do not exactly know where he is now—I do not know what became of the difference of the money—I received the money from my husband, and saw him tie it up myself—I saw the check about eleven o'clock on the Monday night—I did not go with my husband to the Bank—he had to transact some business—it was two dials for his watches—I have them at home now—I saw the money he brought—he never counted it—it was all in gold—I know very little of Leary—I do not think I have spoken to him but once in my life—I never spoke to Jenner before this transaction—my husband has not known him very long—I did not see Jenner about this matter myself—my husband's landlord knows that he brought the check to our house—I saw Mr. Roberts, Mr. Jenner's attorney—I never spoke to him before the 26th of April—Mr. Jenner brought Mr. Roberts to me in the Old Bailey, last Sessions.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What did they want with you? A. They just wanted to speak to me, to know what Mr. Wyatt was going to do—I told them I did not know anything about it—I was advised not to make any communication to Roberts—I think I only spoke to Leary once, that was the night the money was given back—I saw him with Wyatt and the prosecutor then—the only time I spoke to him was while this matter was going on—he has acted throughout to save me and my husband—he is a friend, and an
honest man—my husband has kept out of the way—he has communicated his residence, to come as a witness—I have been married to him fifteen years—he has lived where he does now, for four years, and carried on the same business—he has never been in any trouble or difficulty before—I saw him tie the bag up as he received it from the bankers—in that state I delivered it to Wyatt, at the door—I had not known Jenner—I knew of his calling the same evening that he called.
(Wyatt received a good character.)
LEARY — GUILTY . Aged 34.
WYATT — GUILTY. Aged 29. Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1531]
1531. SARAH RYAN was indicted for stealing 1 basket, value 1 s.; 1 lock, 3 d.; 1 pair of boots, 5 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, 1 s.; 2 aprons, 1 s.; 1 petticoat, 1 s.; 2 pairs of stockings, 8 d.; and 5 3/4 d. in copper; the goods and monies of Honora Collins.
HONORA COLLINS . I am a market-garden woman, and live at Mortlake. On the morning of the 24th of May, I left my boots, my handkerchief, and other things, in the room where I lodged—I had locked them up in a basket—I afterwards met the policeman with the basket and all the things in it—this is it—the things are in it now.
CATHERINE LEE . The prisoner and Collins lodged with me. On this morning the prisoner went out, and I followed her—she had this basket locked up, and her own bundle too—the basket was not broken open.
Prisoner's Defence. I had no work, and I took the basket for a bit of bread; she turned me out, and then she said I was taking away the basket.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1532]
1532. JOHN JENNER was indicted for stealing an order for the payment of 197 l. 10 s.; the property of Edward Ransby.
EDWARD RANSBY . I reside at Bawsey, in Suffolk. On the 26th of April, I came to town to receive money on my father's account—I received a check for 197 l. 10 s., payable at Messrs. Prescott's—this is the check—I had about 18 s. or 19 s. in my pocket besides—I went on that day, about twelve or one o'clock, to the Yorkshire Stingo beer-house in the Blackfriar's-road—I saw the prisoner in front of the bar—he was attending apparently as master—I had a pint of beer—while I was drinking it, a party challenged the prisoner to play at skittles for a sovereign—they went out to play—after they were gone a little while, the prisoner came and invited me to go down in the yard at the back of the house, to see them play—I went and saw them—the landlord won the game—I heard him ask the man for the sovereign—the man said he had left it with his wife in the bar—after that the prisoner asked me to play a game or two of skittles for a pint of porter—I played a game or two—I lost every game—I then played with another man and lost—Leary ( See page 414) was there, but I never saw him play—I played for half-an-hour or three-quarters—before we had finished, several of the men went away—I staid in the skittle-ground—one of them came down and invited me to go up to hear a song—I went up, and after the song, they began to toss for stout and for money—I tossed for stout first, but ultimately for money, with three other men, not the prisoner—when there was a small game I generally won, but when there was a large sum I generally lost—that soon disposed of my 18 s. or 19 s.—I was then going—the prisoner came in and said, "Do not go, you will win your money back"—I said I had got nothing but a check—he
said if I thought proper to trust it to his care, he had got a friend who would cash it in a few minutes—when I had gone in, the prisoner said to me, "You are a Suffolk man," and he mentioned persons whose names I knew—I cannot recollect whether he said he was from Suffolk—he had not lent me any money before he asked me to trust him with the check—he then lent me half-a-sovereign to play, while he went to get the check cashed—that was between four and five o'clock—he said he would get it cashed in a few minutes—he was away half an hour, and when he came back I had lost the half-sovereign, and another one likewise—I was half-a-sovereign in debt to the parties I was playing with—I asked the prisoner to let me have another half-sovereign—he said, "Yes," and he took out 30 s., and put it on the table, and said, "Take that, you will win your money back"—he did not bring change for the check—he said he could not see his friend—he brought the check back—he did not give it to me—I asked him for it back—I asked him if he would take my watch and let me have the check—I had my watch—he was to take that as security for 2 l.—he said, "No, I do not like watches"—he said the check was as safe with him as it was with me, and he told me to meet him in the morning at the bankers—I went on playing till I lost 4 l. or 5 l.—the prisoner still lent the money—as soon as I had lost that, I said I should leave—the prisoner persuaded me to go on further, and ultimately he joined in the game himself, to induce me to play on—I sometimes won a little, and sometimes I lost—I went on till I lost as much as 10 l., for which sum I was indebted to the prisoner—I staid at the house till about eight or nine o'clock—I was to meet him next morning at the bankers, at half-past nine o'clock—I was there by a quarter-past nine o'clock—the prisoner did not come—I waited till about ten o'clock—I then went to his house—he was not at home—I sat down in the bar, and the parties who were there, said he was gone to meet me at the bank—I said, "It is no use my going, he will miss me; I will wait"—I waited about three-quarters of an hour—he came home and told me he had lost his cash-box, with my check in it, and 15 l. or 20 l. of his own money—he did not say anything about the money I owed him—I went to the bankers—I got some of my money back again in the evening of the 27th of April—I had given the prisoner in charge that morning—I got back 191 l. from Leary and Wyatt.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you recollect that evening, that you gave the prisoner the check for the purpose of being changed? A. Yes, that was the object for which I gave it him—he did not tell me when he came back, that he had been to the bank, and was too late—I parted with the check for the purpose of getting it changed into money—I should say I was sober throughout—I am a Suffolk man—I have been in London a few times—I have not been in the habit of going to public-houses—I never played at skittles, or tossed up before in my life—on the following morning, when the prisoner told me he had been robbed of his cash-box, with my check and money of his own, he wanted to go to the Police-station to give information—I did not ask him, before he said anything to me, if he could get the check changed—I only said I had one—I should have left the house—he put that forward that he would get it changed for me—I am not positive whether he produced the check when he returned—this is the watch which I wanted to deposit—it is silver—I went on to borrow a sum of 10 l. in the whole—I afterwards borrowed 10 s., and left the house.
BERNARD BARTON . I am a clerk at Messrs. Prescott's bank. On the 26th of April, about one minute past five o'clock, the prisoner came into the office, brought this check, and wanted it cashed—he was very anxious to get it
changed—I thought he was a countryman, and I was very anxious to do it for him, but we had all closed our books—I did not do it—he said he would give a sovereign for it—I did not understand that he did that for a bribe, but from his anxiety to get it done—the check was presented again the next morning by another person, as soon as the doors were open—gold was given for it.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-constable M 13) On the morning of the 27th of April the prisoner came to the station, he said, "This morning, while I was out a few minutes, my cash-box was stolen from my bar-parlour, containing a check belonging to this gentleman, and three 5 l. notes of my own property."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1533]
1533. MARY ANN MARSHALL was indicted for stealing 1lb. 20 s. weight of bacon, value 1 s., the goods of William Musselwhite; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
HENRY SHIELDS . I am servant to William Musselwhite, a cheesemonger, in Lower-marsh, Lambeth—at eleven o'clock in the morning of the 3rd of June I saw the prisoner take a piece of bacon, and put it under her arm—she inquired the price of several other pieces—I told her the price, and she put them down again—she still held this piece under her arm—she walked away—I stopped her, and told her I wanted the piece of bacon under her arm—she took it form under her arm, and said she was only having a lark, she was only playing—it was my master's bacon.
Prisoner's Defence. I thought I would have half a pound of it; I asked the price of another piece; that was 10 d., this was only 9 d.; he asked which I wanted; I said I wanted the best piece; the wind blew my shawl over it, and he said "I will give you in charge, I think you want to steal it;" I have four orphans; I have been in a bad state of health for ten year; I made away with the last thing I had in the world.
WILLIAM POCOCK ( police-sergeant F 14) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at this Court, by the name of Mary Ann Ryan—( read—Convicted 2 nd Feb., 1846. confined one year)—the prisoner is the person—she had convicted before that.
GUILTY . Aged 46.— Confined Eighteen Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1534]
1534. MARY TAYLOR was indicted for stealing 3 pewter pots, value 5 s. 6 d., the property of Daniel William Saunders.
THOMAS HIGHLAND . I am in the service of Daniel William Saunders, a publican, at Rotherhithe—on the 31st of May, at half-past eleven o'clock I received information—I went out and saw the prisoner—I asked her if she would allow me to look under her shawl—she did, and I found one quart pot—I was taking her back, and going along she dropped two other pots—they were all my masters—she had no business with either of them.
TIMOTHY LEARY . I was standing outside the window—I saw the prisoner sitting on the seat—I saw her take one pot and put it under her shawl—I told the potman of it.
Prisoner's Defence. On the 31st of May I went to Deptford to purchase some articles—I met some friends who had come from Woolwich—we had some ale, and as I had nothing to eat since the morning, the ale made me unable to see—I did not know what I was doing—my friend before starting from me would make me have a drop of rum—they then went home—I had a small basket with some eatables in it—it had no handles, so I carried it in my apron—I fell asleep—I was not sober—I still kept my apron up—I got up and took
a pot which was on the table and was going to the ditch to fetch some water—when I got half way, the potboy came and took the pot out of my hand—he asked what I had got in my apron—I showed my basket—he said, "No, you have got a pot"—he took me back, and after I had passed some persons who were drinking, I saw a man throw a pot off the table, and he said to the boy "There is another for you"—he went and took it—I told him he ought to be ashamed to do it—he said I should pay for all—I cannot account for the post being in my apron, except some boys put it in.
GUILTY . Aged 57.— Confined Two Months.

1536. HUGH SUMMERS was indicted for stealing 7 watches, value 10 l.; the property of Robert Short, in his dwelling-house; and that he had been before convicted of felony; to which h pleaded
GUILTY .* Aged 13.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18470614-1537]
1537. JAMES ATKINS was indicted for stealing 1 cart, value 6 l.; the property of Charles Norville.
FRANCES NORVILLE . I am the wife of Charles Norville—we live at Brixton—I know the prisoner—in May I had a cart for sale—I put it in front of the house—the prisoner pressed me to send it to an auction-room, and said he would take it—I said I would speak to my husband about it—I got his leave, and sent my son up with it—it remained unsold till last Feb., and then the prisoner went to Mr. Lloyd and gave him instructions for it to be sold—I went to Mr. Lloyd, and he had sold the cart by private contract—I have had no money for it—I did not authorize any one to sell it.
CHARLES NORVILLE . I am the husband of Frances Norville—I did not authorize the prisoner to sell the cart—I have not received any money for it.
WILLIAM HENRY LLOYD . I am an auctioneer—the cart was brought to me by the prisoner and a lad—it was deposited with me to be sold at my auction, which was every Thursday—it was not to go under 4 l.—the prisoner said he had been doing business for the party whose cart it was, and he should not like it to go under 4 l.—I sold it and gave the money to the prisoner.
Prisoner. I received the cart on the 19th of May—Mr. Norville's bill amounted to 6 l. 15 s. 6 d., and now his bill amounts to 20 l. 14 s. 6 d.
CHARLES NORVILE re-examined. I do not owe him a farthing—I did not authorize him to sell the cart.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not put the horse in the cart? A. No—I did not know you were going to take it—I thought my son would take it—I never saw you till the following day, when you told me you had been to Mr. Lloyd's—I
did not employ you in Aug., 1845, to get up a fair—I might have been in the field when the attorney and some persons came to take possession.
COURT. Q. But did you employ the prisoner? A. No.
Prisoner. I received 4 l. 7 s. out of 20 l. odd. Witness. I do not owe him a penny.
GUILTY . Aged 42.— Confined Nine Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18470614-1538]
1538. JAMES HURLEY was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 1 l., the goods of Walter Crutchley; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS PARNELL . I live with Mr. Walter Crutchley, a tailor, in High-street, in the Borough. On the 24th of May I received information about a fishing-coat—a woman came in with the ticket which had the price of the coat on it—we then missed the coat from the block.
MARY HOOPER . I am the wife of George Hooper—we live in Falcon-court, in the Borough. On the 24th of May I was standing at the top of the court, and saw the prisoner—he turned the corner very quickly—he had a coat hanging over him arm—I could not tell what it was till he got by me, and then he took the ticket off the coat and threw it away—I took it up, and charged him with stealing it—he ran away—I took the ticket to the prosecutor's, and they missed the coat off the block, which was inside the door.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me take the coat? A. No, I had a suspicion, because you took the ticket off and threw it away—I saw your face—you were closer to me than you are now—you went round to the right—I had seen you so often, that I could not be mistaken in you—I can swear you are the person—you had the coat on your left arm.
HENRIETTA MASTERS . I live in Falcon-court. On the 24th of May I saw the prisoner in the prosecutors' shop—he afterwards passed me with a coat on his arm—I saw him take the ticket off it and throw it down—Mr. Hooper took it up—I am certain the prisoner is the man.
THOMAS HAY ( police-sergeant M 9.) On the 1st of June, after a long run and a severe struggle, I took the prisoner, and took him to the station—he ran off immediately he saw me looking at him—he was identified by one of the witnesses as the man—I have the ticket which I got at Mr. Crutchley's shop.
MARY HOOPER re-examined. This is the ticket I picked up.
THOMAS PARNELL re-examined. The coat that is missed had this ticket on it, and it was the property of Mr. Walter Crutchley.
Prisoner's Defence. Does it stand to reason, that a person walking the street with a coat, could be known by a stranger? I should have had witnesses to prove I was at Stepney fair.
GEORGE WILD ( police-constable M 94) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at this Court—( read—Convicted 21 st of Sept., 1846, and confined six months)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18470614-1539]
1539. ELLEN TAYLOR was indicted for stealing 1 penny, the money of Charles Sutton, her master.
CHARLES SUTTON . I keep the Union Arms, Union-row, Clapham—the prisoner was in my service—I put some marked money on the top of my till—the prisoner cleaned the bar, and I found a marked penny in her drawer up stairs, that was lost last Monday morning—I missed that penny immediately.
Prisoner. A gentleman came, had a steak, and gave me that penny for cooking it; you said you could not swear to it. Witness. This the penny—I know it by some shot-marks on it, and it is bent—I had it from Thursday till Monday—I had not an opportunity of marking it before—I heard the money jink on Thursday, which excited my suspicion—she lent my man 10 s. on Saturday—5 s. of it was in halfpence, which she had no honest means of getting.
JAMES LONG ( police-constable V 316) I searched the prisoner's box, and found this penny—I found one shilling and three halfpence in coppers in the soil of privy—three of the pieces are claimed by the prosecutor as marked—one is an Irish halfpenny, another has eleven dots on it.
CHARLES SUTTON re-examined. I picked them out as being very curiously marked—this occurred early in the morning, before I had done any business—I came down in the morning, and put these into the till—the prisoner went into the bar—I walked to the door—I then came in and missed them—no one had been in the place but her and me.
Prisoner. There was a sovereign I found in your bed, a half-crown I found on the staircase, and a fourpenny-piece; I gave it all to you. Witness. Yes, that is true, that might have been in the way of business—I had no suspicion of her till this.
Prisoner's Defence. I never touched his money; what money I have got I worked hard for; have been with him seven months, and never wronged him of a farthing in my life; I had 11 l. a year; all his books are in the drawer in which that penny was found; all my things and his are mixed together.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1540]
1540. ELLEN TAYLOR was again indicted for stealing 2 pence and 1 halfpenny, the monies of Charles Sutton, her master.
CHARLES SUTTON . I did not examine the contents of the till so as to know what they were—I have not been so particular as I ought to have been—we have generally placed the female servant as a confidential person there—on my charging the prisoner, she went into the yard, and we found in the privy 1 s. 1 1/2 d.—on this penny piece is T. P., on this other, eleven dots, and the other has a mark—the penny-piece I missed on Monday, morning, I put in with the others—there is only myself and my brother living there—he is ill in bed, he cannot identify this coin.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1541]
1541. ROBERT HAINES, DANIEL CONLEY , and JAMES COCKLING were indicted for stealing 800 lbs. weight of iron, value 3 l., the goods of the Southwark and Vauxhall Water Company; and that Haines had been before convicted of felony.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
ALEXANDER CHISHOLM . I am foreman to Mr. Eyer, contractor to the South-wark and Valuxhall Water Company—I was before the Magistrate when some iron water-pipe was produced—it was the property of the Southwark and Vauxhall Water Company—I had taken them up in the Camberwell New-road to lay down larger ones—they were taken up on the Thursday, and I saw them on Saturday, at the merchant's where they had been sold—I know the prisoner Haines, he was labourer to Mr. Hayward—I have seen him about the Company's works.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Haines applied to you for work after he had been discharged by his employer? A. Yes, I intended to make
inquiries about him—these pipes were lying in the road—I know them by the size, having pipes on the spot, and this pipe being gone form the Camberwell New-road.
THOMAS GORE . I am a labourer in the service of the contractor for the Southwark and Vauxball Water Company. On Saturday afternoon, the 15th of May, between three and four o'clock, I saw a van coming along Bowyer-lane with a nine inch branch and a five inch outlet, iron water pipes, in it—I told the driver there was not room to go by—I was shooting a load of rubbish—I saw the van coming, and took my horse's head, and I noticed these pipes in the van—Haines was sitting behind the van—as soon as he saw me he put his hand before his eyes—I had known him by our working at the same farm—there were four persons in the van—I did not take notice of the others—the van was coming form Camberwell New-road—I knew there had been a quantity of water pipes lying in that road—I did not go to where that pipe had been—I gave information to Dooley—the pipes I saw in the van were of the same description as those I saw lying in the Camberwell New-road—I am pretty well sure Cockling was the man driving the van.
Cross-examined. Q. How many person were there in the van? A. Four—I am certain there were four—I did not speak to either of them—it never struck me to do so till the van went by so quick—I was not exactly acquainted with Haines, only by working with him—I did not know either of the others—I have had no quarrel with Haines—he has worked in the same employ as I have—I have put down the pipe, and he has paved the boxes afterwards—he is paviour—that is three of four months ago I suppose—he was getting his living by hard work at the time I knew him—I do not know how it was I did not speak to him on this occasion—I did not notice him till the van had gone by—there were four in the van, and Haines was in the hind part.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Are there any one of the others that you know to be there beside Haines? A. I am pretty sure Cockling was there, but I am not right down sure—the way the van was going, would have led to Bankside.
JOSEPH DONOVAN . I am in the employ of Mr. Plimbey, of Bankside. On that Saturday afternoon a van came, and brought a quantity of iron pipe—Mr. Chisholm came three or four hours afterwards, and claimed some of the pipe that was brought in that van—Haines brought the pipe—he gave me the name of Smith—I bought it of him at 3 s. a cwt.—I did not see any of the other prisoners there—he came to me by himself—he and one of our labourers got the iron out of the van—Haines stated it was his own iron—I know a great many persons who come, but Haines was quite a stranger.
ROBERT BRANFORD ( police-sergeant M 12) I took Haines on Saturday night, the 15th of May—I told him it was for stealing a quantity of iron from the Camberwell New-road—he said he was in the van that day, but he had nothing to do with the iron—I took Cockling on the 17th of May—he said he had been employed by two men to carry some iron from the Camberwell New-road to Mr. Plimbey's.
Cockling's Defence. On that Saturday morning, at half-past seven o'clock, I went to a house in Russell-street, Bermondsey, and moved some goods to Bankside; when I had half unloaded, a man came and said, was I engaged? I said, there was my master; he said he wanted some iron moved form Camberwell New-road, and some men would meet me in Union-street; I went; two men jumped up, and took me to Camberwell New-road, they said that was the iron; I was employed to carry it; my master authorized me to carry it.
Witness for the Defence.
FRANCIS JENNERY . I am a carman, and live at No. 55, Bermondsey-street—Cockling drove my van for about four months, during my illness—on that morning I was unloading a load of goods at Bankside, and I was engaged by an unknown man to fetch some iron from Camberwell—he said the carman was to meet him against St. George's church, to fetch some iron from Camberwell, to bring it to Plimbey's—he did not agree about any price—I left that to the man after he dad done the work—I sent Cockling—he came back about four o'clock in the afternoon, and gave me 6 s.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Where were you unloading the goods? A. The load came from Russell-street to Bankside—it was ordered at half-past six o'clock—they were unloading about half-past nine in the morning—I do not know what morning it was—I did not put it down—it was on a Saturday—a person came up almost against Mr. Plimbey's—he hardly spoke two words to me—I do not know the name of the person from whom I took the goods—it was from a private house—the ladies had gone into the country for their health—they were not unloaded at any house or wharf, but on the shore at Bankside—I do not know whose house they were fetched from in Russell-street—my carman went—I was ill in bed at the time—I am ill now, I am not fit to be here—I got up about eight o'clock—I had my breakfast, and walked to Bankside—I went by myself—they were then unloading these goods—my carman's name is Cockling down here—I do not know whether his name is Conley—I only know him by driving for me—I only know his name is "Cockling" down on the subpœna—I cannot swear whether Haines is the man who came up while we were unloading—I cannot swear to any man—I went up to the Magistrate, but I was too late—I knew my man was an innocent man—I was too ill to go before the Magistrate again—I have been ill these ten months—I never saw the man before or since who came to my carman—I did not notice whether he was tall or short, nor how he was dressed—I did not inquire where he lived, or what he was about—I have no impression whether he might be a gentleman or a common working man—I had but two words with him, and did not notice him—I had no character with Cockling—he behaved very well to me.
JAMES JENNINGS . I am a master mariner—I was in the police—I produce a certificate of Haines's former conviction at this Court—( read—Convicted 23 rd of Oct., 1837, and confined six months)—he is the person.
HAINES — GUILTY .
CONLEY and COCKLING — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1542]
1542. ROBERT HAINES was again indicted for stealing 1,100lbs. weight of iron, value 6 l.; the goods of Joseph Threlfall Carter.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH DONOVAN . I am employed by Mr. Plimbey, of Bankside. On the 15th of May the prisoner brought some iron to our place—a part of it was afterwards seen by Mr. Carter, and Mr. Sutton, the policeman, took possession of it—there was 19cwt. of iron in the van—I paid 2 l. 17 s. for the whole of it.
ROBERT BRANFORD ( police-sergeant M 12.) I took possession of the iron—a part of it was seen by Mr. Carter on Monday, the 17th—he claims a quantity of it—it Mr. Sutton saw it.
GRORGE SUTTON . I have seen a quantity of this pipe in the possession of the policeman—it is the property of Mr. Joseph Threlfall Carter, my master—it
was got from the Well-house, at Sydenham, on Saturday, the 15th of May, by the persons in the van—I saw four men in the van—they came to me as I was at work there between twelve and one o'clock, and said, "We have come for this piping"—I thought my employer had sent them, and let them take it—Conley was one of the men who came—I cannot speak to the others.
THOMAS GORE . I saw the van coming from Camberwell New-road to Bowyer-lane—that would be the way to come from Sydenham—I am quite sure Haines is one who was in the van—I saw a considerable quantity of iron in the van—I did not take particular notice what iron it was—there was a quantity of water-pipe in it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you not swear before the Magistrate that you were quite certain Haines and Cockling were two of the men in the cart? A. No—this is my mark to this deposition—( the deposition being read, stated, "I saw the two prisoners. Haines and Cockling, in the van—Cockling was driving—Haines was in the van—Haines knew me—he put up his arm to prevent my seeing him—I am quite certain Haines and Cockling were the two men.")—I said there were four men—Haines was one—I am pretty well, sure Cockling was the man who was driving—I saw the van about four o'clock.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You gave information of this? A. Yes, to Dooley, whom I was driving rubbish for.
ALEXANDER CHISHOLM . I received information of this from Mr. Plowman.
Cross-examined. Q. Who is Mr. Plowman? A. He is foreman to Mr. Hayward—he told me of this, and that some of our men had been pursuing the thieves.
JOSEPH THRELFALL CARTER . This pipe is mine—I gave no authority to any person to have it removed.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the value of it? A. 7 l. or 8 l.—there are several lengths of pipe of a very remarkable sort—I suppose hardly any of such a sort in the trade—if a person gave 2 l. 17 s. for all mine, and 8cwt. in addition, it is not anything like the value of the pipe—old iron sells very low.
JOSEPH DONOVAN re-examined. Q. How was this iron when it was brought to you? A. All broken up, of no use but for melting again—I should not have bought it for old iron if it had not been broken.
Cross-examined. Q. What time did it get to your place? A. As near as I can recollect, about five o'clock.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18470614-1543]
1543. WILLIAM GEORGE LIPSCOMB was indicted for embezzlement.
JOHN PEEL . I live in the New-cut, Lambeth, and am a printer. The prisoner was in my service as lately as the 20th of April—a person named Snelling was indebted to me for some work done—it was the prisoner's duty to receive money on my account—the course of business was for the money to be entered in the cash-book by the person who received it, the day of the month, and what sort of money was received, and to put the money through a crevice into the desk—I asked the prisoner if Mr. Smith had paid him 9 s.—he said I should find it in the book—I have examined the book, and I do not find it entered—the prisoner has not accounted to me for a sum paid by Mr. Snelling.
Prisoner. The money has been paid to me, and not entered in the cash-book; he asked me if I had received Mr. Smith's; I told him I had; I received the three sums, the 9 s. 6 d., 9 s., and 1 l. 2 s. 6 d., but unfortunately did not enter them in the cash-book.
JOHN PEEL re-examined. Q. How many day's delay was there in the entry in the cash-book? A. I had not questioned him about Mr. Snelling's money—I only asked him about Mr. Smith's—I discharged him for intoxication—I wondered how he got the money.
RICHARD SNELLING , Jun. I am clerk to Messrs. Shield and Co. I knew the prisoner in the prosecutor's service—on the 3rd of April he brought this bill of 1 l. 2 s. 6 d.—I paid him, and he receipted the bill.
CHARLES SMITH . I knew the prisoner as servant to the prosecutor—he brought me a bill of 9 s. on the 15th of April—he told me Mr. Peel was very poorly—I told him to receipt the bill—I gave him the money.
NATHANIEL FREDERICK OKEY . I keep the Hero of Waterloo, in Waterloo-road. The prisoner produced this bill for 9 s. 6 d. to me on the 19th of April—I paid him the amount—he receipted the bill.
HENRY CHAMPION ( police-constable F 107.) I apprehended the prisoner.
JOHN PEEL re-examined. Q. Did the prisoner ever either of these bills in the cash-book? A. He did not—he he did not communicate to me of his own accord that he had received them—he did not admit that he had received any of them, and omitted to enter it—I spoke to him about Mr. Smith's—he said he had entered that—the others I have found out since—he made no communication to me about them—I discharged him about the 20th of April—he did not leave my place till the 24th.
Prisoner. It might have passed my memory; I have been in the habit of receiving money, and always entered it in the cash-book: I have omitted to enter this, but if I had been disposed to have made off with money, I might have made off with more than this; I had a great deal of money; I have delivered this money faithfully up to Mr. Peel. Witness. His duty was to put in into the desk, not to give it to me, but put it down in the book—I compared the money with the book day by day—I did not find there was too much—it always tallied with the book—two or three persons take money—they have order to put down what money they receive, and the description of it—I found no such sums as these in the desk—I found no paper that disclosed he had received this money, not any money unaccounted for—I am able to swear that the 1 l. 2 s. 6 d. was not paid to me in the shop.
GUILTY .— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1544]
1544. MARGARET FLANAGAN was indicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 30 s., the goods of Ahijah Henry Stephens, her master; and JOHANNAH FLANAGAN , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
SUSANNAH STEPHENS . I am the wife of Ahijah Henry Stephens—we live in Surrey-place—Margaret Flanagan was in my service for about five months—I had a character with her. On the 31st of May I went to my drawer for a gown-piece, and missed it—I asked her about it—she denied knowing anything about it—I had her taken into custody, and on the 1sy of June I missed a shawl—her mother, Johannah Flanagan, gave up the duplicate, and the shawl was found at Mr. Russell's, the pawnbrokers—Johannah Flanagan did not come to tomy house—she had noopportunity of taking the shawl—I went to Mr. Russell's, Which is about five minutes' walk from my house; and while I was there, I saw Johannah Flanagan pass—the officer ran and stopped
her—I heard her say that she had told her daughter to take the things from me, and if the officer went home with her she would give up the duplicate of the black satin shawl—she told me that she wanted money, and that she told her daughter to take something from me to get the money.
JAMES COLDMAN ( police-constable M 159.) I received charge of Margaret Flanagan on the 31st of May, at her master's; and I saw Johannah Flanagan near Mr. Russell's shop—I got from her a duplicate of a shawl—she said that Margaret Flanagan had told her her mistress would be in want of the green shawl which was then in pawn; and she told her to get something else to get it out—Margaret Flanagan said that her mother told her to bring things home, and she had taken this shawl home, and her mother had pawned it—this is the shawl—the duplicate was given up to the pawnbroker on account of his giving up the shawl, which the Magistrate ordered me to keep.
MRS. STEPHENS re-examined. This is my shawl.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
MARGARET FLANAGAN — GUILTY . Aged 14.— Confined One Month.
JOHANNAH FLANAGAN — GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined One Year.
The prisoner were recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutrix.
