[trialAccount t18470614-1546]
1546. JAMES LOFTUS BROCK was indicted for feloniously assaulting Ann Brock, and cutting and wounding her on the left arm, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
ANN BROCK . I am the prisoner's wife—he is a lieutenant in the army on half-pay—we were married in 1820, and have three children—he is addicted to drink, and when under the influence of drink he is generally very violent—he has told me that he was not right in his head at times, and to be cautious what I said to him when he was under the influence of drink—he said he did not know what would be the end of it, as he knew his head was bad—we live at No. 105, East-street—on the 3rd of June he came home about half-past ten in the morning—he had been out all night, I think—he was very much agitated indeed—he had been drinking—he went into Mr. Crossland, the landlord's place—I could not hear what was said, but I could hear the tone of his voice—I do not know that he spoke to me—he kept talking to himself for two or three hours—I went down to the yard—he followed me round the yard—I went round because I was afraid he was going to strike me—he had not said anything to make me think so, but I saw he was in a violent passion—he did not say what he was in a passion about—he went up stairs, I remained down till nearly twelve o'clock—he remained quite still, and then I went up stairs—when I got up he came behind me with a knife—I did not see it, but I felt him stab me in my arm—he made no observation at the time, that I heard—I ran out of the room, went down stairs, and got away—I did not hear him speak—my recollection is not more faint now than it was before the Magistrate—he said nothing after he stabbed me—there was nothing said in the house at the time about my life—I do not know what was said out of the house, for the prisoner and the policeman walked on together—my son was in the room, and struck the prisoner's arm—I cannot say in what position the prisoner's hand was then,
as he was behind me—he stabbed me on the outside of my arm, at the back—he said nothing about the children that morning, that I heard—he has wounds in his head, which appear to give greater effect to drink—I am certain he is not right in his head at times—the knife went in at one side of my arm and came out at the other—I hope you will show him as much mercy as possible, I freely forgive him.
JAMES LENIGAR BROCK . I am a son of the prisoner and prosecutrix, and live in the house—the prisoner was out all the Wednesday—he came home about ten o'clock next morning, and went out again almost immediately—I did not see him follow my mother at that time, he went down stairs after her about half an hour after that—he came back in the first instance, and spoke to me—my mother returned up stairs shortly before twelve o'clock—she came into the room, my father went behind her, and stabbed her with a knife in the arm—he had been quiet for a short time previous to that—the knife had been lying open on the table—this produced is it—he usually carried it with him—there was a table in the room, my mother went behind it, and he followed her, and struck at her a back-handed blow with the knife in his right hand—I cannot say what part he aimed at, it took effect on the left arm—I rushed forward and struck his arm as he was in the act of striking—I cannot be certain whether that diverted the blow from the direction it would otherwise have taken—I believe it is the opinion of the surgeon that the blow was struck very closely indeed to the heart—he struck from behind—I do not think he introduced his arm so as to strike before her—he made no observation at the time, but followed her down stairs after he had stabbed her—he then said he wished it had gone to her heart—about half an hour previous to the transaction, he said the flesh was drunk, but the spirit was not—my mother had not been in the room above a minute, when he followed her behind the table—he had been apparently quiet perhaps about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour before she came up—I had heard him use expressions of resentment on other occasions—he has said that when he was in that state of mind he could not be accountable for the consequences, and he could not tell what the consequences would be.
Prisoner. Q. How old are you? A. Twenty-one last November—I have never been from under your roof—I never knew a person to knock at your door and accuse you of acting rascally or dishonestly to us, or of clothing us otherwise than at your own expense—you have nothing except your half-pay.
FREDERICK BROCK . I am a son of the prisoner and prosecutrix—I saw my father when he first came in—he came up stairs—he had no knife then, it was in his pocket, I suppose—about half an hour after he came in, he took it out of his pocket, and ran down stairs after my mother, and ran her up again—the knife was open—when he came up again he shut the knife, and put it into his pocket—my mother remained down stairs till about a quarter to twelve o'clock, she then came into the room again, and went round the table—he followed her round, went behind her, and stabbed her—when he was in the act, my brother struck his arm to ward off the blow—it was a back-handed blow—it pointed towards the fleshy part of her arm—he appeared to be striking towards the left breast, before my brother struck his arm and diverted the blow—at the station he said he wished he had finished her—he was at the station about half-past one—he had been home more than two hours—he had carried the knife in his pocket for two years.
Prisoner. Q. Was I at the station at time I made the observation you allude to? A. Yes; you had made it before—my brother James was with
me at the station—I was seventeen last Aug—I have never been from under your roof—I never knew a person know at your door, and accuse you of being rascally in your conduct, or clothing and feeding us otherwise than at your own expense—you have nothing except your half-pay.
JURY. Q. On what terms did they generally live; did they quarrel? A. His temper has always been very violent, especially when he has had drink—he was affectionate sometimes—my mother never did anything to aggravate him.
JURY to JAMES LENIGAR BROCK. On what terms did your father and mother live? A. He was tolerably quiet when he was sober, but when he was drunk he was violent—he was in the habit of getting too much to drink, I should say, about once a fortnight—when he was not in liquor his conduct to my mother was tolerably quiet and kind—I consider his head is affected—he never drinks spirits, only beer and ale.
Prisoner. Q. Have I ever shown any partiality to one in preference to another of you? A. No.
JAMES COKER ( police-constable L 88.) I was called in about half-past eleven o'clock, and found the prosecutrix's left arm bleeding—the prisoner said he wished it had gone to her heart—he was very much affected at the time—it was not altogether with drink, I believe he was excited—he had the appearance of having been drinking a little, but not to take much effect upon him—I did know him before—on the way to the station he said he should not mind being hung for her—he appeared angry.
Prisoner. Q. Where was I when I said I wished it had gone to her heart? A. In the station, and in going there as well.
EDWARD CROSSLAND . I am a carpenter, and live at No. 105, East-street, Lambeth; I am the son of the landlord. The prisoner, his wife, and three sons lodged with my father—I heard Mr. Brock call out, "Murder!" and they all ran down stairs—when Mr. Brock came down stairs I noticed her arm bleeding—I turned up her sleeve, and found she was stabbed in the arm—when the prisoner came in in the morning I was in the front parlour—when he is sober we are very good friends—he is quiet when sober—I cannot say that, when he is intoxicated; he is violent—I do not live up stairs with them—he has used threats to his wife—the night before this took place he made use of the expression he would throttle his wife—I cannot say whether he was intoxicated then—I was in bed at the time.
Prisoner. Q. Did you ever hear anybody accuse me of acting rascally or dishonestly, or of clothing my children otherwise than at my own expense? A. No.
JURY. Q. Did he state any cause why he should throttle his wife? A. They had been having words—he made no accusation against her.
MICHAEL LAWRENCE MASON . I live at No. 5, High-street, Newington. On the 3rd of June the constable brought Mr. Brock to my surgery about noon—I examined her left arm—there was a triangular wound through the muscles at the back part of the bone—it appeared to have been done by an instrument thrust through—there was one wound above, and one underneath the arm—the cut was continued downwards towards the elbow, almost forming a flap—if it had been continued a little longer the flesh would have been quiet disengaged from below—it did not appear to be made with the same blow, at the same time—there was a stab, and then an extension of the cut, as of a second action of the hand—it might have been done in drawing the knife out, because above the length was two inches, and below, where the knife had passed through, an inch and a quarter—the blow was behind the done—it passed through the muscles—it
went down to the bone—it was through the thickness of the arm—upwards of an inch and a half of the knife went in—the knife produced probably would cause the wound—it is beveled on both sides, which formed the triangle—it was not of a dangerous character—if it had passed in front of the bone it might have been so.
Q. How near the position of the heart was the wound? A. It was in a true line between the fourth and eighth rib.
Prisoner. Q. Could not the appearance that you speak of be produced by a person striking my arm after the wound was inflicted? A. It might, or the extension might be produced by drawing the knife out.
COURT. Q. Supposing the knife to be in the wound, a blow under or above would lengthen it? A. Yes—it might be attributable to that cause, or to the mode of drawing out the knife—I think the arm must have been close to the breast when the side was struck, and that the prisoner stood somewhat backwards—I think it was a back-handed stroke.
Prisoner. Q. Your opinion is that I must have been in front? A. No.
COURT. Q. A person near the arm, but behind, could have made that stab? A. Certainly; but the arm is capable of being moved in so many positions that it is impossible to say where the blow was aimed—I question whether the prisoner is of sound mind.
Prisoner's Defence. I have heard the evidence, but that is all I know of the transaction; I know no more about it than as a dream; my temper is bad at times; my mind has been repeatedly irritated; my wife knows my temper to be hasty; how could a poor lieutenant on half-pay bring up a family if he spent his money in drunkenness? I commit my soul to my God, and my person to my fellow-creatures; this will be depriving me of my half-pay.
COURT to MRS. BROCK. Q. Has the prisoner ever shown any extravgance of mind, or want of balance of mind? A. Repeatedly—he has laid on the bed like a man raving mad, beating the cloths, and tearing himself when intoxicated—when he has been sober he has got up at one or two o'clock in the morning and smoked his pipe at the window, and said, "Oh dear me!"—he has been wounded in the head—he has got a sear—I never applied to any medical man to induce him to put him under any partial restraint—I always considered that if he lived many years he would go out of his mind, raving mad—as he got old he got worse—more than twenty years ago he leaped through a window, glass, frame and all—a person had locked him in—he was then sober—the other day he told me it was a wonder he did not go through the wall instead of the door; and in many other things he has spoken very inconsistently—he reads a good deal, and instructs his children, but he scarcely ever has patience to hear them—he would work himself up in such a way that he could scarcely do it—sometimes he would be in doors a month or six weeks together, and then these fits come on, and he would go out at one or two o'clock in the night.
JURY. Q. Did he complain of his head? A. Yes—if he goes to stoop he puts his hand to his head and says, "Oh my head!"—he would not stoop to do anything—that is when he has been drinking—it has always been my impression that he would go out of his mind—he was very quiet some-times; you would not know a person was in the house, and sometimes his tongue would begin and go on till two or three o'clock in the night, without knowing what he was talking about—he has been to the station-house repeatedly and given information against two policemen—a man who heard his conversation at the window called him a crack-brained fool for the way he was going on, knowing he was not right—it was at certain times, about the new and full moon, that he was worse.
NOT GUILTY, being Insane.
[trialAccount t18470614-1547]
1547. MARY ANN SMITH was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 guard-chain, 4 l.; 1 braquest-chain, 1 l. 10 s.; and 1 sixpence; the property of John William, from his person.
(the prosecutor stated his real name to be Douglas, that he had only assumed the name of Williams in this transaction for the purpose of concealment, and had never gone by that name before.)
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1548]
1548. WILLIAM GRIFFIN was indicted for stealing 6 half-crowns, 3 shillings, and 4 pence, the monies of Samuel Lindsey, his master.
SAMUEL LINDSEY . I am a grocer, and live in New-cut, Lambeth. The prisoner was seven months in my service, and came as a single man, but he is married—he lived in the house till within about six weeks or two months—I frequently missed articles from the stock, and watched a female who came on Saturday the 15th of May, she came to the shop when we were very busy—she did not ask for anything, but tendered him half-a-sovereign—he gave her 18 s. 4 d. out of it—I stopped him, and asked him if he knew her—he said, certainly not, that he never saw her before in his life—she said not ask for anything—he gave her 3 s. 6 d. worth of goods, and gave her 18 s. 4 d. out of a half-sovereign—I charged him with robbing me—the women said in his presence that it was a sovereign she gave him—she bolted off, and never came back—I went to the prisoner's lodging and asked for him—they said he was not within—I asked for Mr. Griffin, they said she was within, and to my surprise I found a wife and three children—I had previously seen two or three sovereigns and two half-sovereigns in the till, and the half-sovereign he put in would make three half-sovereigns—I stood by him, and am certain he took half-a-sovereign from her—I found half-a-sovereign more in the till than there was a minute before—the women left her basket behind her—she went away to fetch a witness to prove she had a sovereign—I found from four to six pounds of sugar at the prisoner's lodging—I cannot swear to it—having received half-a-sovereign, the prisoner pretended to give change for a sovereign—he gave more than the change.
cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. He lived with you four months? A. Yes, from the 20th of Sept.—he lived in the house with me most of the time—he had left off sleeping there—he left my service once, came again in a fortnight or three week, and wished to come back—I took him in, and then he proposed to live out of the house—if I had known he was married I would not have taken him—I cannot recollect whether there were two or three sovereigns in the till, but there were three half-sovereigns—I had looked just before the transaction, and know there were no more sovereigns, and I looked at it afterwards—there was probably 5 l. worth of silver there—it was a little after eleven o'clock at night—I had four persons serving in the shop—there is more than one till—the shopmen would put gold into the till, and take it out if they wanted change—after the woman found that I detected it she said she had given him a sovereign—we had seen her very often at the place—the prisoner was at the bottom of the counter—she came right through the shop down to him—I went and stood next to him—I cannot say now whether I told him to attend to the customer—when we are busy he serves—I sometimes do call him—there were ten or twelve customer in the shop at the time—I stood close to him—what he received from her he dropped into the till—our rule is to put gold on the counter, and take the change out, but he opened the till, and dropped in what he took before he took out the change—I did not change him with receiving it as a farthing—I thought it was a
farthing when it was first given—I did not say, "Was not that a farthing you received?"—I saw it on the middle of the counter when she laid it down—he gave her 1/2 lb. of tea, value 2 s. 6 d. (six ounces and two ounces) and 2lb. of 6 d. sugar—the tea was in separate parcels, 6oz. and 2oz., but it was out of the same canister—he put the change on the counter—it was six half-crowns, three shillings, and four pence—she was in the act of taking it up—she had not taken up any part of it—she laid her basket on the counter, and was going to take it up when I interposed.
JOHN UNDERWOOD ( policeman.) I took the prisoner in charge—I saw a female at the prisoner's lodging—she appeared before the Magistrate—the prisoner said going to the station that he did not know the female, nor was she his wife—after locking him up, Mr. Lindsey and I went to his lodgings, and inquired if Mr. Griffin lived there, they said "Yes"—we asked if Mr. Griffin was at home, they said "Yes," and called her down.
cross-examined. Q. You had not seen the women before? A. No.
MR. LINDSEY re-examined. I saw her at the lodgings, and we had watched her previously to the lodgings, which we knew were the prisoner's.
GUILTY . Aged 45.— confined six Months.
[trialAccount t18470614-1549]
1549. MARY HOLLIDAY was indicted for stealing 9 towels, value 4 s. 6 d.; 1 table-cloth, 2 s.; 8 pairs of stocking, 1 l.; 6 gallons of wine, 4 l.; 30 bottles, 5 s.; 1 plate, 1 1/2 d.; 1 inkstand, 6 d.; 1 pillow-case, 1 s.; the goods of John Spittal Miller, her master.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN SPITTAL MILLER . I live at Camberwell—the prisoner has been in my service as cook about eighteen months. On the 29th of April, in conesquence of information from Ryan, I looked into my wine-cellar, and missed nearly three dozen of port wine—the wine-cellar was kept locked—nobody ought to have had access to it but myself and my wife, who kept the key—I have since seen the key of my garden-gate, and find it opens the cellar—I was not aware of it before—on the 27th of April I called the prisoner up into my room, charged her with having robbed me of my wine, and said if she would confess what she had done, she might go that night—she denied having robbed me at all, and denied having anything of mine—I called in a policeman and she gave him the keys of her boxes—he searched them, and found a table-cloth, towel, and inkstand, three shell, and other articles, which are my property—no wine was found—she was present when the things were found, and said two or three of them were hers—I identify these towels as my property—they have no mark on them, but I know them—I have been in the habit of seeing them for eighteen months or two years, and consider they are mine—here is one marked "M"—I can swear to that as well as to the others—I do not know that particular mark, but I know it is my towel—I recollect seeing this mark on it before—it is just such a mark as mine had—it is just such an "M" as other persons would have whose names began with "M "—the towels were marked with red—this table-cloth is marked "G. R. M."—that is my brother's mark—I know it was in my possession—this inkstand has no particular mark, but I can swear to it—it was kept in the kitchen—this plate is mine, and this cream-jug—these stocking are my wife's—here is a name on them which I wrote myself—the prisoner's box was locked.
cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. was Jane Ryan in your service?
A. Yes—she came in March last—this charge was made on the 27th of April—I have had a cook in my service since I gave the prisoner in charge—she has left—she did not state that she left in consequence of Ryan's conduct—my wife is not here—she has taken no part in the matter—she has kept aloof from it—she could not identify anything—this word, "Eliza," on the stockings is in my own writing—I did not see them taken from the prisoner's box—all the other articles were—this "M" is not my writing—it might stand for "Mary," as well as for "Miller"—the prisoner said the towels and table-cloth were hers—she did not say the inkstand was hers—she said she took it—I have not the least doubt that the towels and table-cloths are mine—I used the towels to wash with—there is no "M" on them by which I know them, but I swear they are mine—I never heard the prisoner swear they were hers—she said the three marked "M" were hers not the others—the prisoner's box was a pretty large one—I believe she has had a legacy left her lately—there was a small box, with money in it, found in her large box—I believe the legacy is in the savings'-bank—I do not know the amount of it—we had a very good character with her—the inkstand was used in the kitchen by the servants—it was sent down for the purpose—I do not think my wife attended to the making up of the linen for the wash—I cannot say who does—here are three different qualities of towels—my linen is generally marked, but not always—some of these things have been in my possession three or four years—I have not a shadow of a doubt about the towel—I know I took six dozen of port out of my wine-bin in Dec. last—there were thirty-eight remaining—the prisoner had two or three boxes—two were searched in my presence—they contained clothes of various kind—she slept on the second floor, the same floor that I did.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Was the box was stairs? A. Yes, in her own room—I know nothing about the legacy except from what she says.
ADA JANE RYAN . I went into Mr. Miller's service on the 23rd of March—about that day I was in my own room, adjoining the wine-cellar—I heard the wine-cellar door unlocked—I thought it was Mr. Miller—I looked and saw it was the prisoner—I heard her hang the key up in the kitchen—I noticed wine in the cupboard some time before that—I dare say there were twenty bottles in a cupboard under the stairs—I mean twenty bottles together, one following the other—they were sometimes full, and used to be taken away when they were empty—I have seen the empty bottles taken away, and have known them to be the same that I have seen there, by the addition to the number of empty bottles in the garden—they were in rows—I saw bottles in the cupboard when I heard the prisoner come from the cellar—I saw a bottle in the cupboard, there had been about one-third taken out of it—I noticed it again afterwards, and there was about half a pint in it—I did not see it again.
COURT. Q. What made you look at the bottles so frequently? A. I had cause to suspect, having seen bottle there a long time—I thought it was not right, but was not certain where she got it from—I never saw her put them there.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. On the day you saw the bottle there, did you notice anything particular about the prisoner? A. She was under the influence of drink—it was the Thursday week previous to Mr. Miller accusing her—I afterwards looked for the bottle, and saw it with the others in the garden—on the Tuesday morning following, I told Mr. Miller—shortly before this I had to look up some towels for Mr. Miller—they were missing—I came down directly to the prisoner, and said, "Ann, Mr. Miller says there are some towels missing and bid me ask you for them" she said, "I do not know
anything of them"—this was, perhaps, a fortnight or three weeks after she came from the cellar—I saw her take this ink-stand away out of the kitchen.
COURT. Q. Did you ever see her write in her room up stairs? A. Never—I brought these stockings down to her—I put them in the back kitchen, and never saw them again till the policemen found them—the prisoner had there boxes, the box in which the stocking were found was not the prisoner's—she used it for her own purposes—it was an old millinery box—the other things which were found besides those produced were hers.
Cross-examined. Q. When were the stockings found? A. On the morning after she was taken into custody—the box was in a spare room adjoining the kitchen—that was not where we used to throw the things for the wash—the box belonged to Mr. Miller—I found the prisoner's bonnet, and some caps, and a lot of rags, in it—it was not locked—there was no lid to it—I came from Yorkshire to the service of Mr. Fletcher, of Camberwell—it did not suit me, and I gave her warning—I was there a month—after that I went as housekeeper to Mr. Elliott, of grove-lane—he is single, and a man of the highest respectability—I was there two months—I was too young for the situation, and did not like to live with him alone—I was not exactly cook enough for him—Mr. Rockwell, whom I lived with three years and a half, brought me from Yorkshire—I have been in no situations in London but what I have named—the cook who came to Mr. Miller's after the prisoner, has gone away—Mr. Miller did not keep the key of the cupboard under the stairs—there was no key to it—I sometimes had occasion to go to it—I never tasted the wine—I am quite sure it was wine—I held it up to the light, and took the cork out to smell it—I am certain I did not taste it—there is a pretty good deal of wine drank in the house—the bottles go into the garden when done with—there is a heap there in rows—I used to count the bottles after my suspicions arose—this was the first time I saw where the prisoner got the wine from—I heard her speak of opening other apartments with keys of her own, and I tried the key to the cellar—I did not try any other apartment—I saw a fresh bottle on Saturday, and one on Sunday—there was only one at a time—I know it was a different bottle each time—my mistress superintends the house—I took up the linen for the wash—the dirty linen is always kept down stairs, in a large bin—it is not taken up to Mr. Miller, and counted out regularly—Mr. Miller never sends a servant to the wine-cellar—I never saw the prisoner drink a glass of wine—I was not much with her—I was very frequently up stairs, or out with the children.
EDMUND DAY ( police-constable P 155.) On Friday evening I was called to Mr. Miller's house, and I asked the prisoner about her boxes—she showed me two, and gave me the keys—I opened them, and found these towels, stockings, and plate—the stockings were in a little wooden box, in the same room when I found them, but they were placed away in another room—the articles in both boxes were intermixed—the first two were locked—I opened them with the keys she gave me—I found all the things in them, except the stockings—it was a small wooden box which was left in the room—I did not examine it particularly that evening—there appeared to be dusters or house-cloths in it, and a bonnet, I think—I examined it more particularly next morning, and found eight pairs of stockings pinned up in an old dirty silk handkerchief—it was then up stairs in the prisoner's room—it had been taken up there by Ryan.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she give you the keys at once when you asked for them? A. Yes—she said the towels were hers—this milk-pot is produced to match with the plate, it is not charged to be stolen—the ink-stand was in her box—I looked at the other box carelessly, and did not see the stockings—it
was in her room that night—it had been brought up there by me and her fellow-servant—next morning when I came I found the stockings in it.
ADA JANE RYAN re-examined. The towels and other were kept in Mr. Miller's wardrobe, but these had not been used by the family while I had been there, which is three months.
COURT. Q. When did you first mention seeing the bottles secreted? A. On the Tuesday previous to Mr. Miller's accusing her—it was on the Thursday before that I found her at the cellar—I was afraid to mention it before, as she had been there longer than I had.
JURY. Q. How long is it since the first of the twenty bottles was seen in the cupboard? A. The first I saw was when I had been in the house about a week, the next a few days, after, and the third a few days after that—a bottle lasted in the cupboard about three days—we have a shilling a week allowed for beer money, and I thought she might have bought it out of that—I am away from the kitchen so much that I could not watch where she got the wine from.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1550]
1550. HENRY SMYRKE was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Charles Clarke and another, at St. Mary, Newington, and stealing therein 2 shawls, value 3 l. 13 s.; 1 half-shawl, 1 l.; 24 handkerchiefs, 3 l.; 8 neckkerchiefs, 1 l. 10 s.; 3 scarfs, 13 s.; and 637 yards of silk, 60 l. 7 s.; their property.
JAMES WICKENS . I am carman to Chaplin and Home, and live in Cornbury-street, Old Kent-road. On the 3rd of May, about a quarter-past six o'clock in the morning, I saw two men walking backwards and forwards in the Dover-road, close to Mr. Clarke's shop—one of them took off his top coat, hung it before his legs, and close by the area grating—the other one came behind him, lifted up the grating, and somebody from below put out a large sack—the one who lifted it up put it on his shoulder, and walked towards Napier-street—the prisoner was not one of those men—he was about fifteen yards from the place with a cab —he saw the men coming with the bag, opened the cab -door, and the person who had it put it into the cab —I informed the police—I had seen the cab standing there for about five minutes—the prisoner could see what they were doing, if he was looking.
Prisoner. You stated before the Magistrate that I was half-way down Napier-street. A. I said your cab was a little way down the street—I do not know the length of Napier-street—I believe you were fifteen yards off Napier-street—I was about 11 yards from the shop, and you were about two yards down the street.
WILLIAM RANDALL ( policeman.) On the morning of the 15th April I received information, went towards Napier-street, and saw a cab which the prisoner was driving towards Kent-street—I saw a man running alongside the cab —I called to the cab -man to stop—I was in my police dress—he was near enough to hear me, but drove on—the man by the side of the cab went off in another direction—I followed the cab, stopped it in Long-lane, and asked the prisoner what he had got—he said he did not know—I asked where he had got it from—he said it was put into the cab in Napier-street—I asked by whom—he said by two men who employed him in Aldersgate-street—I took him to the station, and found the property stated, in the cab —I produce it—Napier-street is about twenty yards from Mr. Clark's shop—there are four houses in Napier-street.
Prisoner. Q. When you stopped me was not I a long way down Long-lane? A. Yes; I did not stop you myself, you pulled up—when I asked where you got it from, you said two gentlemen put it into the cab at the corner of Napier-street—I did not ask what you were going to do with it—I got on the cab, and you drove on—you made no resistance.
JAMES WILES WHIBLEY . I am an assistant to Charles Clarke, a draper, in the Dover-road. On Thursday morning I went to the station and found these goods, I know them to be my employer's—I had locked the premises the night before, about half-past nine o'clock, and went away—I did not sleep there—next morning I was called up by one of the young men, and found the shop had been entered by the grating window, through the area, the silk all taken off the rollers, and the shawl drawer open—the maid-servant had been in the shop before me—she lives in the house—she is not here.
Prisoner's Defence. Two gentlemen ordered me over the water, and told me to wait in Napier-street till they came to me; they brought a cab, and told me to drive over to Bermondsey Church, and took my number at the same time; I drove off, and when the policeman called me I stopped—a person two yards off cannot see the house.
WILLIAM RANDALL re-examined. I can see the house twelve yards up thestreet.
JAMES WICKENS re-examined. I could see the house and the grating fromwhere the cab stood—when I saw the prisoner I was in the Dover-road.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470614-1551]
1551. FREDERICK HAGGETT was indicted for feloniously breakingand entering the shop of Richard Waygood, at St. Mary, Newington, and stealing 5 metal taps, value 15 s.; and two copper boilers, 30 s.; his property.
RICHARD WAYGOOD . I live in the Blackfriars-road, and have a shop at Newington-causeway. I have a back entrance leading to Tibberton-street—when you go in there you come into a yard, and another door leads into theshop—I know the doors were all locked up at eight o'clock the evening previous—both the gates were locked, one comes into the yard, and one into the shop—I went to the shop about six o'clock on Saturday morning and found the front door broken open—I afterwards examined the back door, which was forced open—I missed two large copper boilers and a lot of brass taps which I had seen safe the night before—I have not seen them since—I should know them again—I sent a fac-simile of the coppers to the police—the prisoner worked for me about twelve months at the manufactory.
WILLIAM BOSTICK . I am an engineer, and live in Tibberton-street—I work in the next premises to the prosecutor. On the 9th of April I left my premises about ten minutes after eight at night—I know the back gates which Mr. Waygood speaks of—I locked them that night—we both have access to the yard—I took the keys with me—I went next morning at a quarter to six; found the box of the lock wrenched back, and the wicket gate open.
CHARLES WATERS ( policeman.) On the 10th of April, about twenty minutes to six in the morning, I was in William-street, about 150 yards from the prosecutor's premises, and saw the prisoner coming in a direction from there with a copper boiler under his arm, similar to the one produced—I noticed the peculiarity of it, and heard something ratling inside it—I thought he was going to his work, and did not stop him.
WILLIAM DAVIS ( policeman.) On the morning of the 10th of April I saw the prisoner in St. Ann's-road, carrying a boiler similar to the one produced, and he had a bag and something in it.
RICHARD WAYGOOD re-examined. The copper produced is like the one I lost.
JURY. Q. Does anybody else make such boilers? A. Not to my knowjedge.
WILLIAM HARDEN . I saw the prisoner outside the prosecutor's premises, from half-past five to six o'clock.
NOT GUILTY .
